## The trend is not always your friend

Forecasts have run aground during this expansion when previous business cycle regularities have been applied to an unprecedented post-pandemic environment. Extrapolating a weakening in global goods sector activity in the face of aggressive monetary tightening fueled recession fears as we turned into 2023. While reliably signaling forthcoming weakness in the past, an unusual element of the recent goods sector slide was a normalization of demand components (namely tech and inventories) that were elevated in the early stages of the pandemic recovery. Meanwhile, the transmission of the monetary policy drag to the broader economy was being blunted by unusually positive cyclical momentum building in the services sector.

In a similar fashion, market participants have seized on inflation<sup>’</sup>s inertial properties in extrapolating an impressive 2H23 slide. The downshift in core inflation was narrowly concentrated in goods prices, however, where the removal of bottlenecks, falling commodity prices, and weak goods demand generated a synchronized global slide in core goods CPI growth (Figure 1). For some months, this global disinflationary impulse has been fading as goods demand has picked up and input costs have firmed. Forecasting the trajectory for service price inflation is more difficult and subject to idiosyncratic developments. Nevertheless, strong growth in service sector activity, tight labor markets, and elevated labor costs gains argue against a follow-through of last year<sup>’</sup>s goods price slide to service sector disinflation in 1H24.

This assessment underlies our view that global and US core CPI disinflation will stall in 1H24, with the pace of gain remaining at a still elevated 3%ar. This stall has been expected to delay the start of the DM easing cycle to midyear, and curb enthusiasm about the overall magnitude of the easing cycle ahead. The broader business cycle implications of slower and shallower easing are less clear.

Regional Data Calendars

Sustained restrictive monetary stances are incorporated in our “boiling the frog<sup>”</sup> narrative that recession vulnerabilities gradually build. But the DM has displayed surprising resilience, most notably in sustaining balanced profit and labor income growth. Alongside supportive financial conditions, it is possible that neutral policy rates are higher than we appreciate and that a high interest rate soft landing can be achieved. Just as we need to be careful in extrapolating momentum, inflation data are inherently noisy and monthly surprises need to be interpreted with caution. In this regard, the firm January core readings posted in the Euro area (0.3%m/m) and US (0.4%) likely overstate underlying inflation. But looking though this noise, we extract two signals from last month<sup>’</sup>s inflation news.

Core goods disinflation is ending. Excluding a large drop in US used car prices, January inflation reports point to a broad stabilization in global goods prices. Importantly, this message is consistent with the signals from business surveys and trade prices. The US has seen a sharp rise in imported consumer goods prices as we turn into the new year, even as China continues to exert a deflationary impulse (Figure 2).

Service price puzzles are fading. Globally core service price inflation aligned with our forecast as we moved through last quarter, stabilizing at an elevated 4.2%ar. However, Euro area core HICP and US “supercore<sup>”</sup> (services ex rent) core PCE delivered significant downside surprises. We have faded these moves and January releases point to a significant rebound in both cases. In the US, the swing is dramatic. We currently anticipate a 0.5%m/m rise in core PCE prices in January, which should lift core PCE supercore to a 4.4%ar in the last three months (Figure 3).

Figure 3: US "supercore" inflation
![](images/185f26f7d20abf702b6a6168cef57155eaf0a251a06bdc237b1b82b8ad4adcb7.jpg)

Last week in these pages we noted that modest, correlated changes in the macroeconomic outlook can have a significant impact on central bank behavior. While it is premature to place significant weight on noisy January data, risks have shifted in the direction that core inflation and labor market conditions both surprise the Fed in a hawkish direction in

1H24. In the coming weeks, it will be important to gauge the follow-through of these developments in the February data and the reverberations at the March FOMC meeting where 2024-26 projections will be updated.

## Tracking the year-end goods sector fade

We have been looking for a broadening in the moderate recovery in global factory output that took hold last year. Thus far much of the lift has been concentrated in Asia, fueled by a strong rebound in tech spending. The January PMIs point to upward momentum as we turn into the new year, a signal we expect to be increased by a rise in next week<sup>’</sup>s February flash DM PMI. But the survey level remains low and incoming IP data point to some fading strength. Specifically, Europe is still largely treading water and the EM contracted outside of Asia. The US has been a regional outperformer, but IP growth slipped in January. Indeed, outside of a recovery in motor vehicles from earlier strikes, US manufacturing output has declined for four consecutive months through January.

The anticipated goods sector recovery relies on consumer spending gains, which saw a large boost from the US last year. But the US consumer goods demand engine—which generated a 5.9%ar gain in 2H23—looks likely to slow and spending will need to pick up elsewhere. Unfortunately, consumers<sup>’</sup> pocketbooks remained closed in Europe and Japan, which was apparent in disappointingly weak GDP reports for 4Q23 this week. Both Japan and the UK have recorded two consecutive quarters of declining consumption. While details are not available elsewhere, weak consumption has likely contributed to last quarter<sup>’</sup>s GDP stall in the Euro area and Central Europe. We remain optimistic that consumer goods demand will broaden across regions this year, given favorable fundamentals such as solid labor markets and elevated saving rates. However, the large decline in goods prices, which has also been a tailwind, may be fading as an additional support this year. A reported large 7.2%m/m, sa slump in global motor vehicles sales in January adds to the concerns.

## Two-sided risks keep BoE cuts in August

Although UK GDP contracted last quarter, we think the balance of recent data releases support our call for a later BoE cut in August. A strong January gain in retail sales highlighted recent volatility in the hard data, and we think the economy is stagnating rather than contracting. In addition to signs of a housing upturn, momentum signs from some of the softer data (including surveys) have been hinting at a nascent pickup in growth; we expect next week<sup>’</sup>s PMI to signal improvement in 1Q. The latest labor market report also signaled firm pay growth and a vacancies-to-unemployed ratio that has stabilized at still high levels. While inflation surprised to the

downside, the decline was narrowly concentrated and leaves the underlying pace looking firm—all of which should make the MPC more cautious about starting to ease absent signs of clearer progress.

## BoJ macro/market dilemma re-emerges

Rising divergence between financial markets and the real economy likely poses a dilemma for the BoJ: a hawkish pivot could create headwinds for an economy already showing signs of lackluster demand, while a dovish lean would induce further undesired depreciation of the yen. The 4Q23 GDP print marked a second consecutive quarter of negative growth and confirmed our concern that slow wage increases are holding back domestic demand. At the same time, the combination of the strong performance of the US economy and the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s slow walk toward policy normalization has led the yen to break 150 per USD and the stock market to mark its highest level since 1990. This strong market performance, alongside the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s signals that it had been gaining confidence in the inflation outlook, has led market participants to anticipate an early removal of NIRP. We think it will be difficult for the BoJ to justify further curbing demand through higher interest rates to tame inflation—especially as inflation, too, has moderated recently.

![](images/86c2f04bbc629a42ef48b9adb27a38ef9dd17c45ccfa744f8f831d2a99463856.jpg)

## China: January credit report card

Credit jumped 9.5%oya in China in January, implying a strong gain on the month but consistent with the usual strength at the start of the year. Supporting this outcome was a rebound in loans, while bond issuance was solid (Figure 4). But there are two important caveats. First, the credit report confirmed that private business demand remains weak as loans are being channeled to infrastructure, post-disaster relief, and public housing. Second, last week<sup>’</sup>s Monetary Policy Operation Report suggested TSF growth will likely moderate in coming months, to around 9%oya in March. This slowdown appears most likely to occur in loan growth rather than government bond financing.

Next week provides a window for policy rate decisions on the MLF rate on Sunday and the LPR rate on Tuesday. Our baseline is for two 10bp cuts this year, with the first in March. But as January inflation surprised to the downside, we do not rule out an earlier cut this month. In addition, central government bond yields have been below the 1-year MLF rate since January, which has been a typical precursor of policy rate cuts in recent years.

## South Africa’s elusive fiscal consolidation

An envisioned fiscal improvement for South Africa last year has not materialized as the deficit looks to have widened to more than 6% of GDP (including Eskom support). Softer commodity prices hit revenues, but the lack of expenditure discipline has been surprising—even for an election year. This puts an emphasis on next week<sup>’</sup>s budget to include fiscal reforms to improve the medium-term outlook, including an expenditure review process, measures to steady the tax ratio, and new fiscal anchors. A comprehensive macro policy review may also be presented, which could strengthen support for inflation targeting (allowing the SARB to move to a point target, which could then be steadily reduced). This change would anchor inflation expectations, and perhaps offset concerns of a more unorthodox proposal to draw down unrealized FX revaluation gains to fund the fiscal deficit.

## Bringing forward rate cuts in Romania

Despite sticky core services in Romania<sup>’</sup>s January CPI report and a hawkish statement by the central bank earlier this week, Governor Isarescu suggested the easing process could start as soon as April or May—provided the Board sees “at least two months of disinflation.<sup>”</sup> While we had flagged a risk of earlier easing following soft inflation prints during 4Q23, this statement solidifies our expectation for a move in May (rather than our previous August start date). We now see the NBR cutting 75bp (from 50bp previously) to 6.25% this year, with some risk of a larger 100bp of cuts. In either case, with excess liquidity being removed at the deposit rate, the effective policy rate is currently 100bp below the official rate. As we forecast inflation at 5.5% for end-2024, we think an effective 5% rate represents the lower bound for policy this year.

## Nowcast of global growth: Another small step up

The J.P. Morgan forecast for 1Q24 global real GDP growth remained unchanged this week at 2.4%ar (Figure 1). We continue to look for US growth to moderate from its above-trend 2H23 pace to 1.75%ar, while the rest of the G-4 economies are expected to accelerate from their recent growth stalls. Underlying these moves are divergent paths for consumer spending. In the US, where consumption grew 3.0%ar in 2H23, this week<sup>’</sup>s retail sales and inflation data suggest some slowing in momentum to begin the year. In the UK and Japan, meanwhile, weakness in household spending led to 4Q23 GDP contractions, the second consecutive quarterly decline for both economies. Going forward, we expect improving consumer sentiment and real income gains to lift consumption growth across Western Europe and Japan, while the same supports should help spending remain resilient (if moderating) in the US. We<sup>’</sup>ll look to next week<sup>’</sup>s DM flash PMIs for more insight.

Figure 1: J.P. Morgan global GDP
![](images/7e4765becdcead2a96095e7b4a170a981e9304b19c58ffa62a015d60f5a37b3e.jpg)

The top-down global nowcaster estimate for 1Q24 global real GDP ticked up again this week, rising 0.1%-pt to 1.9%ar. The estimate now suggests 0.5%-pt of downside risk to our forecast (Figure 2). The bottom-up nowcaster meanwhile slipped 0.1%-pt, moving about in line with our forecast for the same country set. The nowcaster continues to see upside risks to growth in China (+1.8%-pts) and downside risk in the US (-1.0%-pt), but now sees risks about balanced in the Euro area (-0.2%-pt).

In this week<sup>’</sup>s Daily Economic Briefings, we updated our global consumer goods spending and manufacturing output aggregates through December. Global factory output (ex Ireland) rose 0.1% on the month, with the three-month run rate moderating to 1.2%ar. We look for solid consumer fundamentals to support a consumption-based lift in factory output in 1H24. Recent demand signals, however, have been mixed. Global real goods consumption looks to have lost steam into year-end, slipping 0.2% on the month as gains in the US and China were offset by broad-based weakness elsewhere. Our global auto sales aggregate also showed a 7.2% monthly drop in January. Although this downturn was primarily concentrated in sizable US and China contractions, the rest-of-world aggregate also fell 1.6% on the month.

## Global house prices: Turning a corner after the correction

Global house price gains have moderated considerably in response to last year’s jump in rates

However, house prices continue to rise for most, and at a still-solid pace for many—including the US

In contrast to demand-driven surge ahead of the GFC, the latest house price surge owes to lack of supply

• Post-pandemic house price surge raises bubble concerns; in the US, the gain is twice the pre-GFC run-up

Healthy fundamentals temper exuberance concerns, but loss of labor income could spark sharp correction

In the wake of the pandemic, global house prices surged at their fastest pace in 40 years, spurred on by low interest rates, increased demand, and reduced supply. Since then, a fading of the post-pandemic bounce along with aggressive central bank tightening has put a halt to the run-up. However, while the pace of house price gains has collapsed over the past year, the latest data suggest this disinflation trend is already turning a corner. Global housing prices appreciation ticked up 0.5%-pt to 1.7%oya in 3Q23, led by an increase in the US (Figure 1).

![](images/4050387f8cfd6a7a025ef351fc87683a559ee7581826a8334428a6ef04a18117.jpg)

The moderation in house price appreciation from its torrid pace in 2022 reflects efforts by central banks to cool economies and slow consumer price inflation. Across the DM, policy rates have surged 400-500bp, with mortgage rates following a similar path. As a result, global house price appreciation slumped to a modest 1.2%oya through mid-2023 after peaking at 10.9%oya in 1Q22. The risk has been that house prices slip into outright contraction, a rare outcome only seen following the Global Financial Crisis in 2008-09.

However, the tide now looks to be turning. Increased optimism around a soft-landing has buoyed expectations for 2024 rate cuts. This has led, in turn, to a fall in mortgage rates (Figure 2). Though the US 30-year mortgage rate remains up

nearly 4%-pts, the 110bp decline since late October is already having a positive impact on some of the US housing demand numbers. House prices are showing a similar positive reaction. Since reaching a trough in 4Q22, house price appreciation has steadily increased. The latest available data show a 0.4%-pt rise to 3.1%ar in 3Q23. If sustained, the sharpest rate hiking cycle in decades will have only slowed growth in house prices without generating a contraction (globally and in the US).

Figure 2: US interest rates
![](images/0d660c458805e8eb6106bdbb9d745954147ee744ee7c4cadcdb3c3ca6915f6c3.jpg)

The building expectations for coming rate cuts have also buoyed broader financial conditions. Global equity markets rebounded in the second half of last year, as global real GDP grew at an above-trend 3.1%ar in 2H23 (Figure 3). Though we expect GDP to return to a more trend-like 2.2%ar in 1H24, the robust gains in equity prices and turn back up in house prices reinforce the health of the household balance sheet and, in turn, support continued gains in consumer spending.

Figure 3: Equity and house prices, global
![](images/51d6c4b2bb9d4d4411d6d8fa26ecf7d5b8e7b7755b610e1bbb7da4c1adcc454e.jpg)

While housing affordability is still a problem, the backdrop differs from the conditions ahead of the Global Financial Crisis (GFC). Balance sheets are healthier, leverage is lower, and financial conditions are set to be easing. Elevated house prices owe to a lack of supply, rather than a demand-driven price bubble. The solution for this is more housing construction, which should increase as rates decline over the coming year.

## House prices continue to rise

Despite moderating significantly from the boomy gains of 2022, appreciation in house prices in many countries remains solid—or even robust (Figure 4). In the DM, the US is the standout performer. While US gains are well below their 18.7%oya peak in 1Q22, house prices continued to rise last year—advancing a strong 6.4%oya. Prices in Australia are also up notably through 3Q of last year, rising 5.2%oya. House prices remain on the rise in Japan as well even if still on a moderating trend. Elsewhere in the DM, prices are correcting downward. The latest housing price data show contractions over the year through 3Q23 in the Euro area (-2.2%), and through 4Q23 in the UK (-2.3%) and Canada (‑0.8%).

Figure 4: House price growth
![](images/89d7c42df6c511ad4c949db405bae0e48fcb5e77d36fb914c7ca31f5145b79ce.jpg)
<sup>tur co</sup>l <sub>m</sub><sup>ex po</sup>l u<sup>sa</sup> <sub>a</sub><sup>us</sup> i<sup>nd</sup> r<sup>ou</sup> <sub>s</sub><sup>gp ph</sup>l j<sup>pn</sup> i<sup>do</sup> <sub>h</sub><sup>un</sup> r<sup>us</sup> b<sup>ra zar</sup> <sub>m</sub><sup>ys</sup> <sub>c</sub><sup>he</sup> <sub>c</sub><sup>hn</sup> n<sup>or</sup> <sub>c</sub><sup>an ch</sup>l <sup>mu</sup> g<sup>br</sup> c<sup>zk</sup> <sub>s</sub><sup>we nz</sup>l k<sup>or</sup> p<sup>er</sup> Source: National sources, J.P. Morgan. Details on request.

Across the EM, house price appreciation remains elevated. Mexico and Colombia continue to see the largest gains (outside of Türkiye, where %oya inflation rose to 65% last month), with Mexico in particular sustaining above-10%oya growth for the last four available quarters. Appreciation rates through 3Q23 are also elevated in Poland (9.2%oya) and Romania (4.7%oya). In EM Asia, a slight 3Q 0.1% fall in China was partially offset by gains elsewhere. Notably, the Korean housing market correction continued in 2023 after a contraction in 2022.

## For most an acceleration, for some a lot

The sharp slowing in house price growth in the wake of rapid tightening in financial conditions in 2022-23 raised concerns that the housing market would be thrown into a tailspin. The latest data suggest that not only have house prices continued to rise in most countries, but have actually accelerated across nearly all—lowering the risks of a shock to the housing and lending sectors of the economy.

![](images/db27b3005abc4b70526ed905677c1670ba5b36b6908aba424c5e4c924ff9d47d.jpg)
Source: BIS, OECD, national sources, J.P. Morgan Economics. Details on request.

Again, the US stands out. Not only did house prices rise last year, but the pace of increase has rebounded in the last two quarters—from 5.6%ar in 1H23 to 7.2%ar in 2H23. But even more broadly, for most countries the pace of growth over the two-latest-available quarters is at or higher than the prior two quarters, suggesting a stabilization in the housing market adjustment (Figure 5). For the majority of DM economies outside the US, the latest two-quarter changes remain at or below zero. However, the rate of housing deflation is moderating. In the EM, India, Romania, and Chile saw a particularly large reacceleration in house prices, while only Peru, Malaysia, and Thailand saw a further notable slowing in the pace of price changes.

## A crisis of supply, not demand

The resilience of house prices despite a surge in mortgage rates makes for a somewhat unique housing cycle. This is particularly so compared to the GFC, when rising rates sparked a material housing correction. The difference owes in large part to the contrast in the types of shocks underlying the two episodes. In the 2000s, boomy demand (aided by easy lending standards) was met by a surge in construction. In the late stages of the bubble, demand cracked, leaving the market with a large excess of housing. In the US, vacancy rates spiked in 2006-07 before crashing back in the wake of the GFC (Figure 6); in the current period, vacancy rates are at multidecade lows.

Figure 6: US vacancy rate and existing home sales
![](images/7ce063fb90f8df5743d510643755c766fa642337a113a4bfda07503701fe7f7f.jpg)

In contrast to the 2000s surge, a lack of supply rather than demand seems to have driven the latest rise in house prices. The shortfall of houses on the market reflects both falling existing home sales and a stall in the construction of the new homes. This lack of existing home sales owes primarily to high rates that have locked people into their lower-cost mortgages. In turn, the slowing of churn in the existing markets also slows new construction for builders of new homes. Given higher material costs, difficulty in getting skilled labor, and rising costs of land and regulatory challenges, residential investment has remained sluggish across the DM (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Residential investment per capita
![](images/a1955c9a02f66ab3452b5ceb75fd01139b07b945b84566d9b079395e1600fe50.jpg)

## Potential bubble concerns…

The source of house price resilience coming from a supply shortfall does not necessarily preclude a more damaging correction. For this, a better understanding of the fundamentals is required. While the turn back up in house prices is encouraging, the elevated levels remain a concern for housing affordability and sustainability. On the heels of such a strong price spike after the pandemic, the reacceleration is raising the specter of the Global Financial Crisis. Given the central role of housing in the 2008-09 crash, the bubble concerns are understandable. In the DM, prices have risen most in the US, where the gain since the start of the expansion is larger than in the lead-up to the GFC. Since 1Q19, US house prices are up 57% as of 4Q23 (Figure 8). This compares to the 29% gain in house prices over the corresponding period from 1Q03-4Q07. Put simply, house price gains since the pandemic have been roughly twice as large as the largest housing bubble in modern memory.

Figure 8: US house prices
![](images/130798c3e165daf2e7aa041b971667d5058e6b194ed0aef62c64983dc61605de.jpg)
Source: FHFA, J.P. Morgan

Across the DM, only the US has surpassed its pre-GFC house price experience (Figure 9). House prices across Western Europe, in particular, have not seen as much of a run-up over the past four years. This owes to the past year and a half of house prices correcting downward. However, for those economies where house prices continue to rise, including Australia, the comparison to the pre-GFC period looks more similar. Regardless, the GFC may be too high a bar. Even if not as extreme, the challenges posed by house price affordability and sustainability are a concern.

Figure 9: House prices, change pre-GFC and post-pandemic
![](images/b6bc85def28d11c5d91a13ec93f9bc688d159c0d7bf5be383120948d1702899f.jpg)

## …but fundamentals more reassuring

Assessing this risk of a housing bubble needs to recognize the numerous factors underlying the fundamentals of house prices, and thus the sustainability of the housing sector more broadly. The balance of these factors suggest house prices are elevated but are not raising the same type of alarms seen ahead of the GFC. Specifically:

HPIs elevated but moderating. The house price to income (HPI) ratio is a key gauge for housing affordability. The HPI surged in the 2000s as house prices outpaced gains in incomes. The decrease in affordability was a red flag for the coming crisis. A similar rise in HPIs was seen in the aftermath of the pandemic as a rotation occurred away from cities and toward owner-occupied housing at time when construction was limited. For just about every DM country, the post-pandemic surge lifted HPIs to levels higher than pre-GFC (Figure 10). For six of the nine DMs we track, HPIs remain higher now than at the peak of the pre-GFC period. However, the cooling in housing markets since the surge in policy rates has relieved some of the pressure—HPIs are off their recent highs across most countries.

Figure 10: House price to personal disposable income ratio % deviation from 2012-19 average (HPI is per capita)
![](images/e72049cae7b3b741e2a7622dbd4cccfe2a1766ca0110cd243607434b33bd0d5d.jpg)
Source: Dallas Fed, national sources, J.P. Morgan. Details on request.

Leverage elevated but less than GFC. A key factor underlying the sensitivity of households to rising rates is leverage. For the largest DM economies (the US, EMU, and UK), household leverage is lower now than just before the GFC (Figure 11). Combined with longer duration debt, we have argued in other research that debt servicing costs are more limited (here). For the DM as a whole, both leverage and debt servicing costs look on par with the levels seen early in the 2000s expansion and not like the levels seen on the eve of the GFC. Balance sheets in Australia and Canada stand out however as a potential concern.

Figure 11: Household debt-to-GDP, latest and pre-GFC
![](images/5b45b53d7210ff7f6c3e4f5cb463aee5731051c60c988dc98d2d05f171388ebd.jpg)

No signs of irrational exuberance. A more thorough examination of house prices relative to their fundamentals is provided by the Dallas Fed.<sup>1</sup>The measures look for bubble-like trends in house price relative to fundamentals.

We focus on the price-to-income relationships. These exuberance indicators all spiked over the pre-GFC period. And while they also perked up after the pandemic, all have moved considerably lower over the past year even as rates have moved higher (Figure 12). This owes in part to the health of balance sheets but also to the strength of labor incomes supporting debt servicing costs.

Figure 12: House price-to-income exuberance indicator Statistic; dashed black line denotes 95% confidence upper bound
![](images/26a660165c84c768e1411ac98576d3e055d162bed9c2708003bcad0ff36f00cf.jpg)

Given the more constructive backdrop for the housing market now than during the lead-up to the GFC, the current house price rally looks more sustainable to us. With financial conditions now easing—including a fading in the pace of tightening credit standards and broader financial stress (Figure 13)— we think the threat of a housing shock looks limited.

Figure 13: G4 credit standards and global financial stress index
![](images/12bae25cef00b01a3c86022e758ffab3ca085807216e6e98d81c399e8d8d642d.jpg)

Up to now, the health of labor markets along with strong balance sheets has not led to forced selling that would rapidly deflate prices. This further differentiates the current experience from the GFC. However, should a recession come, the risk would be for a more substantial house price correction.

## Climate change: Bad and could be worse

• January saw record global mean near-surface temperature, the 8th monthly record in a row

• Standard climate models can accommodate this, but some models suggest more rapid temperature change

Hansen argues IPCC underestimates climate GHG sensitivity, cooling impact of aerosols, and lags

The coming years will be material for this debate through both climate and new satellite data

The scientific consensus on climate change as reflected in the work of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) is worrying enough. That states greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions need to reach net zero by mid century to limit man-made temperature change to 1.5C above pre-industrial levels. It is not at all clear we are currently on that path. Meanwhile, 2023 was remarkable in terms of global mean near-surface air temperature (Figure 1). That is not just because it saw a new record high above the 1.5C threshold set in the Paris Agreement, but also because it exceeded the prior (2016) record by a large margin. Data for January report an eighth consecutive monthly record.

Figure 1: Global Surface Temperature Deviation from 1951-80 average, degrees celsius
![](images/65593745eb3da001018607e4cf58a7a6c80a5c83723b392dc3e2ff0255c3126a.jpg)

Even given that 2023 was an El Niño year, recent temperature outturns have been a large upside surprise. The data lie within the confidence intervals of projections from mainstream climate models. However, those intervals are wide based on the historical variability of the data. As record temperatures are sustained, attention is directed toward models which suggest larger or more rapid temperature change than the consensus. One such model comes from James Hansen and his co-authors.

Hansen is a former NASA scientist who came to prominence via his warnings on man-made climate change to US congressional bodies in the 1980s. He has long argued that the scientific consensus is underestimating the likely impacts of

human activity on earth systems. He and his coauthors have recently challenged the IPCC on three specific points. First Hansen argues that the sensitivity of temperature to GHG accumulation is at the very top of the range of estimates. Second he argues that aerosol pollution has been lowering temperatures more than realised, masking the temperature impact of GHG emissions. Third, he claims that long lags mean a large proportion of GHG-induced temperature change is “in the pipeline<sup>”</sup> and will occur even if emissions are reduced quickly. We discuss each of these claims in turn.

## An ECS of 4.8?

The Equilibrium Climate Sensitivity (ECS) is the long-run change in global mean near-surface air temperature associated with a doubling of CO2 concentration in the atmosphere. While often discussed as a single number, it reflects the outcome of multiple systems working within climate models, acting as a summary of how the model behaves. The spread of estimates for ECS has been reasonably stable in work since the 1970s, with a minimum of 1.5C and a maximum of 5C, but with most congregating near 3C.

A key input into ECS estimates is data on the history of the earth<sup>’</sup>s climate, and the extent of the swings in temperature as CO2 concentrations have varied in the past. Models are typically calibrated to be able to explain that history. Hansen highlights recent research suggesting that the swing associated with the transition from the last glacial maximum (LGM) some 20,000 years ago to the preindustrial Holocene was significantly larger than previously thought. On that basis he argues for an ECS of 4.8, at the very top end of the range of estimates.

## Aerosol pollution as a net coolant

Small particles of matter (aerosols) are released into the atmosphere as a result of various industrial processes. These can interact with cloud formation and solar radiation in a variety of ways. On net, however, such pollution tends to reflect radiation back into space, helping to cool the planet. This has served as a partial offset to the heating effect coming from GHG accumulation, which prevents heat being radiated back into space. What is at issue is the magnitude of this effect.

Hansen argues that while the effect of higher GHG on climate has been underestimated (ECS is high), the offsetting impact of aerosols has been understated. He cites experience with the removal of sulfur from shipping fuels as evidence for a large aerosol impact on climate. Regulatory requirements on the sulfur content of marine fuels were tightened in 2010, 2015 and 2020. Satellite data report that cloud coverage and the amount of solar radiation being reflected back into space changed markedly over major shipping routes as sulfur emissions were reduced. Hansen argues humanity has been striking a “Faustian bargain<sup>”</sup> with the climate, whereby one form of pollution (aerosols) has been limiting the observed impact of another (GHG). But aerosol pollution itself has major negative impacts on human health; as it is curtailed the extent of the increase in temperature driven by GHG will be more clearly revealed.

## Lags in the pipeline

According to most climate models, the linkage between an increased stock of GHG in the atmosphere and higher global temperatures plays out very quickly in geological terms. Much of the temperature impact of higher GHG is visible within a handful of years, and the full impact within a couple of decades. The implication is that if the stock of emissions stops growing, the man-made boost to global temperature should also stop increasing shortly afterward.

Hansen argues that the impact of a large climate forcing effect, like increased GHG, is not just larger but takes a much longer lag to feed through into observed climate outturns. The vast majority of Earth<sup>’</sup>s imbalance between incoming energy and that radiated out into space is absorbed not by heating of the air, but by heating of the oceans. Hansen argues that heating of the oceans generates much greater inertia in climate systems than is commonly understood, with warming of the seas likely to generate feedback effects into a warmer climate over a longer span of time. Hansen argues that while the rate of global climate change is accelerating currently, some 7-10C of temperature change is already “in the pipeline<sup>”</sup> over the next 200 years given the increase in GHG emissions that has already occurred.

## Controversial or clear-eyed?

Taken together, the implications of Hansen<sup>’</sup>s views are frightening. He argues that collaboration between the West and China is vital to bring GHG emissions down, and that humanity will ultimately be forced into attempts to reverse the geoengineering it has undertaken through GHG via interventions like solar radiation management. Each of the elements of his argument are vigorously contested among climate scientists, and the claim that geoengineering beyond emissions control will be needed is very controversial. Hansen is critical of the IPCC and what he regards as the dangerous complacency of much of the scientific community. While scientists close to the IPCC are respectful of Hansen<sup>’</sup>s contribution to raising awareness of climate change, they tend to be dismissive of his more recent work.

## No one said this would be easy

For the non-specialist seeking to understand current climate debates, there are at least two difficulties. First is that what we observe as aggregate climate systems are the behavior of myriad moving parts, each with their own specialist disciplines and uncertainties. Although one can attempt to simplify how these behave as an aid to understanding, the need to understand and quantify complex underlying relationships remains. Although there are many accepted quantifications and results, one does not have to dig very deep to find multiple areas of debate and ongoing research, each of which can potentially have significant effects on the behavior of climate systems as a whole.

Second, scientists themselves can be subject to forms of “groupthink<sup>”</sup> or bias, and sociopolitical forces will have shaped the way the conclusions of climate science are expressed. This can make it difficult to assess how much weight should be given to “outlying<sup>”</sup> views. Significant components of Hansen<sup>’</sup>s views could survive a process of scientific peer-review, even if many dispute his overall conclusions. To some extent the IPCC process is designed to distill a scientific consensus to inform a policy response, and it is possible dissenting views have tended to be suppressed in that process.

## Where policy and science diverge

The “good<sup>”</sup> news is that a couple of developments suggest Hansen<sup>’</sup>s arguments will be tested against the consensus soon. First, Hansen<sup>’</sup>s models suggest that, rather than reflecting random variability around a linear rising trend, recent high temperatures will soon be revealed as part of an underlying acceleration in the rate of temperature change. In his words, “The 1.5C degree limit is deader than a doornail...For the rest of this decade the average is going to be at least 1.5C<sup>”</sup>. Second, while all of the issues raised above are active areas of research, one where data are more limited is the impact of aerosol pollution on the climate. A few days ago, NASA launched the PACE (Plankton, Aerosol, Cloud, oceanic Ecosystem) satellite. This promises a new stream of both high and low frequency data which will allow that issue to be better understood.

Many climate scientists expressed disappointment at the outcome of COP28. And in Western democracies at least, the last few months have seen efforts to combat climate change on the back foot. Climate policy has become a point of divergence between parties ahead of the US presidential election. Protests by farmers have caused environmental protections in the EU to be removed or weakened; the UK has been delaying some climate objectives. There is clear pushback against efforts to combat climate change, even as the scientific consensus is that efforts to address climate change must accelerate. But there is at least a possibility that views akin to Hansen<sup>’</sup>s begin to be vindicated, suggesting more rapid climate change than thought, and more intensity to scientific warnings and demands for a policy response.

## US: The remarkable growth of the disabled workforce

• Persons with disabilities account for almost one-third of the labor force growth over the past three years...

• ...even though they only account for 11%-12% of the adult population

Both the business cycle and policy have likely supported this surge in the participation rate of the disabled

• International comparisons suggest this dynamic could have further to run

Arguably the most welcome economic surprise of the last three years has been the recovery in labor force participation. Persons with disabilities have been central to this recovery. While the labor force participation rate of persons without disabilities is still below pre-pandemic norms, the rate for persons with disabilities has climbed about 4%-pts relative to that earlier period (Figure 1).

Figure 1:Labor force participation rates by disability status %, sa, both scales
![](images/ca2f999b52cf95c1d3027ac3f14acd8be57319a47ca152f0daf40e9bf9d64230.jpg)

Put another way: over the past 36 months the labor force participation rate is up 1.2%-pts; one-third of that increase, or 0.4%-pt is due to persons with disabilities, 0.8%-pt is due to persons without disabilities.

Figure 2: Labor force growth
![](images/72c629e5f7d32e4a6c61612582ab5ecbc2a0d7684364cb72ab7ca919c52b35b6.jpg)
Persons with disabilities account for only a fraction of the overall population, recently around 11%-12%. But even so,

the increase in their participation rates has had a significant effect on overall labor force growth. In the three years ending in January the civilian workforce increased by 7.194 million; 2.318 million were persons with disabilities, or 32.2% of overall labor force growth in that period (Figure 2).

## Good news, but why?

So what accounts for this remarkable contribution of the disabled to labor force growth and to the overall economic recovery? Any answer to that question will have to be at least somewhat conjectural. The regular monthly data on labor market outcomes by disability status only begin in 2008 and don<sup>’</sup>t contain much granularity. What little granularity it contains—by gender—indicates both men and women participated about equally in this increase in participation (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Participation rates of persons with disabilities %, sa, both scales, 16 to 64 years
![](images/ae4036d5144f4ada81160182a4a80c6048ab1b87295bb2b8058016641cf80584.jpg)

Data limitations notwithstanding, it seems uncontroversial to think that the very tight labor market of recent years must be an important part of the story behind the increase in the participation rate of the disabled. Businesses have always had reasons to encourage hiring of the disabled, but that drive was almost certainly boosted by an environment in which jobseekers are scarcer than job openings.

The composition of the disabled may have also shifted in a way that is more amenable to employment. The data on the employment status of the disabled comes from the Current Population Survey—more commonly referred to as the household survey. In that survey respondents are asked about a few conditions that could affect their daily activities. One of those questions is “Because of a physical, mental, or emotional condition, [do you] have serious difficulty concentrating, remembering, or making decisions?<sup>”</sup> Given this inclusion criterion it<sup>’</sup>s possible that so-called “long Covid<sup>”</sup> disabilities may account for the modest increase since 2021 in the share of the population self-classified as disabled. If these individuals have greater attachment to the workforce then a composition effect would support the participation rate of the overall disabled population.

Increased prevalence of telework opportunities may have marginally contributed to the increase in participation: 24.5% of workers with disabilities teleworked last month, versus 22.8% of those without disabilities.

On a longer horizon, there are also reasons to believe that policy may have also played a role. With few exceptions federal disability insurance benefits are only available to those without a job, which may disincentivize job-seeking among beneficiaries. As a share of the eligible population, federal DI recipiency peaked in 2013 and has since steadily declined (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Persons receiving disability benefits % of population ages 25-64
![](images/a4840ee997e9bea94c25cb23f5183169c2673966fe501a66f8bfbf3a974f1632.jpg)

Some of this decline was the result of a policy choice. Beginning in 2009 the Social Security Administration, which administers federal disability insurance, took steps to retrain administrative law judges in ways that reduced the frequency of awarding disability benefits.

A complementary change in policy took place around the same time when Medicaid coverage was expanded to most adults with income up to 138% of the federal poverty line. This is significant because about one-third of the value of federal disability benefits is through early access to Medicare coverage. The combined effect of these changes should reduce the marginal tax of shifting into the paid workforce.

Another plausible reason for the improvement in the participation of the disabled may be changes in the treatment of physical disabilities. In a shocking and influential 2017 paper, late Princeton economist Alan Krueger claimed that almost half of the decline in prime-age male participation rates over the prior 15 years was due to increases in opioid prescription rates. Moreover, this opioid effect is conditional on workers having a disability. After the CDC first declared opioids an epidemic in 2011, and under the pressure of increasing regulatory and legal scrutiny, opioid prescription rates have declined (Figure 5).

Figure 5: National opioid prescription rate per 100 persons
![](images/518dc05082b2deba2a0c26703b2dde6fa3c9c2769d8b456d72c4037d52f7c081.jpg)

Presumably Krueger<sup>’</sup>s logic should work in reverse, as more enlightened treatments should—perhaps with a long lag— improve labor market outcomes for the disabled.

## Room to run

Structural changes in work arrangements, income support programs, and therapeutic interventions may all contribute to a trend improvement in participation rates for the disabled. But the sharp improvement over the last few years likely owes to the high-pressure labor market. In this case, demand created its own supply, and prospects for increased participation hinge on prospects for the expansion. Looking beyond the expansion, further improvement in disabled participation rates could yield huge dividends. While international comparisons aren<sup>’</sup>t perfect, looking north across the border indicates that even after the recent improvement there is scope for further gains (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Employment rates, persons with and without disabilities
![](images/fcf979034bb73e69b0f576029719978aefa1ae50b5f849782f50844cd8650963.jpg)
If the US gap in employment rates between persons without and with disabilities narrowed to levels seen in Canada, that would add 8.5 million persons to the workforce.

## Israel: Shun no labor

• Israel<sup>’</sup>s labor market softened in recent months...

• ...although unemployment remained steady, the output gap likely turned negative

Labor market indicators helped gauge depth of economic decline in Covid, can be useful now, too

LMCI signals a 15%q/q, saar, GDP decline in 4Q23; our forecast is -12.0% (previously: -10.0)

Unemployment is not always the best representation of labor market conditions. Although unemployment in Israel has remained steady post October 7th, beneath the surface both labor demand and labor supply were affected significantly. To capture broader labor market developments here we construct a labor market conditions index (LMCI). The index may be helpful in calibrating our estimates of the output gap and in tracking economy<sup>’</sup>s ups and downs during large shocks like war or the pandemic. Unfortunately, however, the index is not very helpful in explaining inflation dynamics in past 15-20 years (nor is unemployment).

## Labor market softened

Israel<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate remained little changed since the onset of the war with Hamas, staying in 2.8-3.1%, sa, range during 4Q23. Reports suggest that the draft (more than 300,000 called at the peak), and reduced numbers of foreign and Palestinian (blue-collar) workers have led to labor shortages in construction and agriculture. However, other indicators suggest that labor market conditions may have softened in 4Q. The vacancy rate dropped to 2.4 in 4Q from 2.6 in 3Q (Figure 1), while the share of workers temporarily absent from work jumped to 13.5% of labor force from 6.6%. Labor force participation slid to 62.6% from 63.5%. Unemployment fears in the consumer confidence survey jumped to levels comparable to the pandemic episode (Figure 2). An indicator aggregating information from a few available labor market series could be a useful gauge of the actual state of the labor market.

Figure 1: Unemployment vs. vacancy rate
![](images/f61105733e34b5437d0be9775e7f0c9c99d0beae6e5d13ff0eda15b1344ff045.jpg)
Source: CBS, Haver, J.P. Morgan

Figure 2: Labor force participation rate vs. unemployment fears
![](images/b3ff845b70bf10fcf398f7d8489acf8e215a03d03b927d7debafd5a7b64adbf2.jpg)

## Introducing LMCI

Researchers have experimented with various composite labor market indicators, which aggregate information from a broad range of labor market indicators via dimension-reducing techniques like dynamic factor models or principal component analysis (see here for an overview). Such indicators usually work as a useful additional gauge about the economy<sup>’</sup>s position in the business cycle, overcoming the imperfections of single indicators like the unemployment rate. Indeed, the unemployment rate displayed only modest variation during the two huge macroeconomic shocks of past four years — the Covid pandemic and the Israel-Hamas war.

Table 1: Variables underlying the LMCI
<table><tr><td>Variable</td><td>Adjustment</td><td>PC1 loadings</td></tr><tr><td>Unemployment rate</td><td>HP, standardized</td><td>-0.09</td></tr><tr><td>Labor force participation</td><td>HP, standardized</td><td>0.43</td></tr><tr><td>Working part-time</td><td>Standardized</td><td>0.33</td></tr><tr><td>Temporarily absent from work</td><td>Standardized</td><td>-0.48</td></tr><tr><td>Vacancy rate</td><td>HP, standardized</td><td>0.42</td></tr><tr><td>Average workweek</td><td>Standardized</td><td>0.34</td></tr><tr><td>LT unemployment (&gt;27 weeks)</td><td>HP, standardized</td><td>-0.14</td></tr><tr><td>Unempl. without job in past 12m</td><td>HP, standardized</td><td>0.37</td></tr><tr><td>Foreign labor</td><td>HP, standardized</td><td>0.14</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan

We approach this task by aggregating nine time series, which start from at least 2006 and should be useful in displaying labor market softness or tightness, by means of principal component analysis. All series were made stationary by detrending using a HP-filter.

Beyond the unemployment rate itself, we use the following variables: labor force participation rate (should be higher when economy is stronger), share of part-time employment in total employment (higher), share of those temporarily absent from work in total employment (lower), vacancy rate (higher), average workweek (higher), share of long-term unemployment (>27w) in total unemployment (lower), share of those who did not have job in past 12m in unemployment (higher), and the share of foreign workers in total employment (higher). Signs of component loadings into the first

principal component (Table 1) coincide with our priors. The first principal component, which we name the Labor Market Conditions Index (Figure 3), explains about one third of the variation of the underlying series.

Figure 3: LMCI vs. unemployment rate
![](images/a1046a2d624a640e748bf494a79988b1a1b80d00aae22a7a6ce78cda1c9d958e.jpg)

## A window on the output gap

As the chart above shows, the LMCI displays substantial cyclical variability around the main recession events of the past two decades, namely the GFC, Covid pandemic and Israel-Hamas war. It is therefore tempting to interpret the LMCI as a useful alternative gauge of economy<sup>’</sup>s position in the business cycle, similar to the output gap. Figure 4 shows the trajectories of the output gap based on Hodrick-Prescott filter applied to GDP series and the LMCI — they look similar. As some of the underlying series behind the LMCI were also HPfiltered, some co-movement is probably to be expected, but versions of the LMCI based on other de-trending procedures look alike, too.

Figure 4: LMCI vs. output gap (HP-filter)
![](images/bf9af5bf8e39008579fbab922da9f188ce02d9f7d15f2cf7598058ccecb143cb.jpg)
Figure 5: Output gap measures % of potential GDP\*

Making a step further, we can also use the signal from the LMCI and, combined with the GDP series itself, extract the estimate of the output gap via a Kalman filter. As with the HP filter, the caveat is that a wagging tale at the end of the sample may be an issue (Figure 5). If the recent LMCI dynamics are any guide, the economy softened notably in 4Q23 and the output gap likely turned substantially negative — perhaps to the tune of 3-5%. That said, the gap has already started closing from late last year as the economy and the labor market have been recovering rapidly. A word of caution is due, however.

![](images/a77ee53b2be45614d917c6929535584f5c6cc7e7dc7be7e34994528ee997b5a7.jpg)

One difficulty in interpreting the recent moves in LMCI is related to the nature of the shock, and the policy response. Although weaker economy was an obvious driver of labor market in 4Q, security concerns and physical constraints to mobility, as well as working-from-home schemes have likely reduced not only labor demand but also labor supply, and could have affected some the the underlying LMCI indicators in unexpected ways. For instance, a lower share of foreign workers likely indicates not (only) the demand weakness, but also authorities<sup>’</sup> decision to limit number of work permits for palestinian workers, as well as foreign workers<sup>’</sup> security concerns, i.e. reduced supply. The scale of the move in LMCI therefore may not be as indicative about this amount of slack in the system in current recession episode.

## A Phillips curve, maybe?

Another word of caution about the LMCI is that it does not improve our understanding of the link between inflation and labor market slack in Israel. To be sure, the unemploymentinflation link has weakened around the world in recent decades, with Israel being no exception. Still, simple specifications for consumer inflation or nominal wage inflation show that the input of labor market to inflation has been insignificant since 2006, with regression results being not robust to specification (Table 2).

Table 2: Phillips curve OLS regressions, 2006-23
<table><tr><td colspan="4">Dep.var.: CPI, %3m, saar</td><td colspan="2">NWage, %3m, ar</td></tr><tr><td>Const</td><td>-0.69***</td><td>-0.44*</td><td>0.35***</td><td>0.37***</td><td>2.83*** 2.71***</td></tr><tr><td>CPI(-1)</td><td></td><td></td><td>0.83***</td><td>0.81***</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>NomWage(-1)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>0.20***</td><td>0.21***</td></tr><tr><td>InflExp12m</td><td>1.58***</td><td>1.43***</td><td></td><td>-0.59</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Unemp_gap(-6)</td><td>-0.83***</td><td>0.16</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>LMCI(-1)</td><td></td><td>-0.12</td><td></td><td>-0.08</td><td>-0.96**</td></tr><tr><td>LMCI(-3)</td><td>-0.08</td><td></td><td>-0.12***</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>NEER R^2</td><td>0.42</td><td>-0.12** 0.41</td><td>0.70</td><td>-0.12*** 0.69</td><td>0.04 0.07</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan

Figure 6 makes this point differently by showing the contribution of the LMCI to inflation dynamics derived from a VAR model — it has been minimal outside of the Covid period. To be sure, the very volatile nature of Israel<sup>’</sup>s CPI data makes any modelling challenging. Still, our tentative conclusion is that although the LMCI may help frame the discussion about Israel<sup>’</sup>s business cycle and the output gap, it may not necessarily improve our ability to predict inflation. However, it can probably be helpful in guesstimating the level of economic activity, especially at times of large swings in activity.

Figure 6: Contribution of LMCI to inflation
![](images/0e82626ee769923c395affcb473b2e623337e11e46d748ca089dfd8e1aa2a53e.jpg)

## GDP nowcasting

We suspect that the war and Covid shocks affected the labor market similarly, affecting labor demand and supply. In both episodes, physical constraints to activity and a confidence shock meant a large fall in economic activity. LMCI can be a helpful addition to other indicators to scale the likely impact of war shock, and we have been using it to guesstimate the depth of economic decline in the 4Q23. It has been showing a slightly larger hit to the economy in 4Q compared to more standard nowcaster, which uses a wider range of economic indicators (22 in total, but only 2, unemployment and vacancy rate, from the labor market).

We have back-tested the quality of nowcast and LMCI-based GDP forecasts starting from 2016, with three assumed cutoffs: at the first week of each month of the quarter in question. For instance, we looked at which data set was available in the first week of Jan.2016 (1st month) to build a nowcastand LMCI-based prediction of GDP in 1Q16, then same was done for Feb.16 (2d month), Mar.16 (3d month) and the following quarters. Aggregated forecast errors are shown in Table 3 below (the lower the RMSE, the better are forecasts).

In the pre-Covid period, LMCI-based predictions of GDP growth were roughly on par (or as bad) with nowcaster. And, interestingly, quality of forecasts was not improving as we moved through the quarter. Indeed, when looking at chart of Israel<sup>’</sup>s q/q, saar, GDP growth pre-Covid, the impression is often that this macro variable lived its own life, fluctuating somewhat randomly and independently of other variables

(yes, even in calm times, forecasting Israel<sup>’</sup>s GDP is hard!).

Table 3: RMSE of GDP forecasts by nowcaster and LMCI
<table><tr><td>Model</td><td>1st month</td><td>2d month</td><td>3d month</td></tr><tr><td colspan="4">Forecasts for the period of 2016-19</td></tr><tr><td>Nowcaster</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td>LMCI</td><td>3.2</td><td>2.8</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td colspan="4">Forecasts for the period of 2016-23</td></tr><tr><td>Nowcaster</td><td>8.4</td><td>7.3</td><td>6.7</td></tr><tr><td>LMCI</td><td>7.8</td><td>6.2</td><td>6.7</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan

However, during the Covid episode — both recession and recovery — the LMCI was providing some gain relative to the nowcaster, especially early in the quarter. (Note that RMSEs in full sample are much higher vs. pre-Covid period due to sheer size of Covid-related swings in GDP in 2020- 22). In other words, amid large economic dislocations the LMCI was a relatively good GDP predictor. Hence using it currently may be of value. Our latest LMCI-based estimates suggest that GDP may have contracted 15.0%q/q, saar, in 4Q23, before bouncing 14.6% in 1Q24; the nowcaster shows -14.3% in 4Q and +8.8% in 1Q (Figure 7).

Figure 7: GDP nowcasters
![](images/c4ece94a3a8fccd88fdf681120867ee083fb924e9b59a8379a2a0dfc00ac8063.jpg)

With LMCI in hand we have revised our near term GDP forecasts somewhat. Our current GDP growth forecast for 4Q23 stands at -12%q/q, saar (previously: -10.0). We suspect that increased activity in the military and defence sector and government consumption, which are poorly captured by any high-frequency data, might have partially offset the weakness elsewhere. We forecast GDP to bounce 9.0%q/q, saar, in 1Q24 (previously: 6.0). Our full-year forecasts for 2023 (2.6) and 2024 (2.2) remain somewhat above those of the BoI (2.0 and 2.0) and the MinFin (2.0 and 1.6).

Table 4: GDP forecasts
