## RBA policy now looking Taylor-made

Taylor Rule estimates of the appropriate RBA cash rate are falling with inflation

Taylor Rules should align with the actual cash rate by mid-year, having been significantly above throughout this cycle

The data argue against higher U\*, while higher r\* would require a shift in the bearish narrative on productivity

Australian inflation is still above target, but moderating. If the RBA<sup>’</sup>s inflation and unemployment forecasts are realized, at mid-year Taylor Rule recommendations for the policy rate will align with the current cash rate for the first time this cycle. Though we do not think the RBA should, or does follow such prescriptions, the convergence of Taylor Rules toward actual policy settings is significant for market perception of risks.

Many who argued for more aggressive policy settings did so on the basis that real rates were too low, and the policy rate should respond more than one for one to inflation shocks, as a Taylor Rule would also advocate. But the bottom-up analysis has suggested otherwise as the labour market is obeying longestablished norms, so was not a significant extra source of cost-push inflation. Policy has curtailed demand (Figure 1) and so should bring inflation down gradually, normalizing real rates over time. The 4Q CPI data seem to verify that path.

Figure 1: Unemployment and GDP growth
![](images/dbf53f482a805e19bcab120412f172921881014e5da3249e7a81f786e5a55e81.jpg)

Below we outline how Taylor Rule prescriptions are evolving, as the data moderate. This still requires judgements, as Taylor Rules need assumptions for U\* (natural rate of unemployment) and r\* (neutral real rate). But there doesn<sup>’</sup>t seem to be much scope for tinkering with those assumptions greatly, given the signal from related data. First, there is little evidence to suggest U\* has gone up in Australia, and our/RBA/Treasury estimate in the low 4s is not far from the current unemploy-

ment rate. Similarly, while the fiscal policy outlook has hawkish implications for RBA policy, it doesn<sup>’</sup>t obviously feed through to higher neutral rate estimates. Particularly large increases in neutral rate assumptions tend to require stronger potential growth assumptions, which would also challenge the bearish consensus narrative on productivity.

## (Don’t) Be real

On the official quarterly measure, inflation peaked at 7.8%oya in 4Q22. The cash rate was 3.1%, implying deeply negative ex post real rates. As Governor Lowe argued at the time, that is not a fair representation of policy settings when inflation expectations are relatively anchored, not to mention when many factors aside from the level of interest rates were driving inflation. That argument was broadly vindicated as inflation has moderated considerably with real rates only turning positive in 4Q23.

Figure 2: RBA Cash rate and Taylor Rules
![](images/17fa50f65229d6193577864b104fc37349ea47c03372fe28106c1d6b0c307830.jpg)

Taylor Rules are a commonly referenced tool for assessing policy settings (Figure 2), with similar features to the real rate prescription above. There are many forms, but most use the inputs: r\*, current inflation, the gap between current inflation and the target, and the gap between unemployment and U\*. Taylor<sup>’</sup>s (1993) original rule for the US had a 1.5x weighting on inflation, and 1x weighting on the unemployment gap. A later iteration also referenced by the Fed - the “balanced<sup>”</sup> Taylor Rule - has a 2x weighting on the unemployment gap. Though designed for a different economy, the balanced rule has similar weights on inflation and unemployment as implied by the policy rule in the RBA<sup>’</sup>s Martin DSGE mode (which is specified in changes, has more terms, and requires mapping from growth to unemployment).

The Taylor Rule recommendations peaked at 12-13% cash rates, depending on the specification. Policy has therefore been consistently and significantly easier than Taylor Rules suggested. This is a reasonable approach if large but temporary non-monetary shocks were also at play in driving inflation. Similarly, over the last year RBA officials have repeatedly described policy as “restrictive<sup>”</sup>, despite real rates

remaining negative, as they have also viewed inflation as being only partly driven by prior interest rate settings. With inflation coming down and unemployment having risen from the lows, on the RBA<sup>’</sup>s recent SoMP forecasts these Taylor Rule recommendations for the cash rate will drop to around 4.2% at mid-year, below the current cash rate for the first time this cycle.

## U\* appears low...

The original and balanced rules above give similar recommendations despite different weights on unemployment, because the unemployment gap term (U-U\*) is currently, in our view, small. That wasn<sup>’</sup>t always the case (see mid-2020), which highlights that tinkering with weightings only makes a difference if unemployment is some distance from U\*.

Of course, we still need a specific U\* assumption, and we are using 4.25%, as we did pre-pandemic. This assumption is informed by consistent features of the economy over the last decade. An unemployment rate in the low 4s empirically suggests wage growth will stabilize in the mid-3s, which means (after productivity adjustment), unit labour costs growing in line with the 2-3% inflation target band. Those established relationships may seem in flux, but however inflation expectations are formed, sustained overshoots are most likely to challenge those expectations when unemployment is significantly below U\*. So at face value, relative stability in inflation expectations and convergence of inflation back toward the target are consistent with unemployment being relatively close to U\*. That again implies $\mathrm { U ^ { * } }$ is quite low.

## ...or at least, not rising

To bring in the data and test U\* more concretely, there is a technical, but still pertinent question of what we want U\* to represent. The theoretical Phillips Curve defines $\mathrm { U ^ { * } }$ as the level of unemployment where inflation expectations are confirmed. So where do inflation expectations come from? The assumption that households have adaptive expectations (i.e., trace recent inflation) seemed close to correct when inflation was evolving gradually and looked like a random walk, which is the case for most of the last few decades. It is also fairly indistinguishable from anchored expectations, when inflation is close to the target. And, it covers the dynamic central banks fear most when inflation is very high: that this will become embedded in expectations. Then, inflation would keep accelerating until something (interest rates or otherwise) brings unemployment back up to U\*.

Assuming adaptive inflation expectations, a crude $\mathrm { U ^ { * } }$ estimate can be obtained by also assuming NAIRU is relatively stable (while unknown), and that unemployment is independent of other noise and supply shocks hitting the economy. In that case, NAIRU is the ratio of the intercept and slope terms in a Phillips Curve regression (see Ball and Mankiw, 2002). Applying this to Australian data since 2016 delivers an estimate of 4.2% (orange line in Figure 3), close to our baseline view.

Figure 3: Rough NAIRU estimates, 2016-onward
![](images/4d54fb4958cd2e95e154d20596c55d3112d6b70cc4cdce36f4181bf8fc308ae2.jpg)

That estimate will be biased if for example there were persistent positive supply shocks lowering inflation, as occurred from 2016-2020. Using a separate set of (older) estimates for average Phillips Curve shape from the RBA (here), we can derive a time-varying estimate of U\*, along with the supplyside noise that surrounds it, if expectations are adaptive. Separate identification of U\* distinct from the noise is not possible from this method, but we can examine how this <sup>‘</sup>noisy U\*<sup>’</sup> is changing over time. As shown in the blue line in Figure 3, this exercise does not support the idea that U\* has risen lately. If inflation expectations were truly adaptive, inflation actually rose less, then fell faster than would be expected from the peak, which implies U\* would be lower, not higher.

Figure 4: Australian (Price) Phillips Curve since 2016
![](images/f416421ecb452e16d65ce09a4cb84316fd3002a74026739b208d48489dfad66e.jpg)

Turning to a more general treatment of inflation expectations, we note that it is difficult to distinguish between a Phillips Curve that is genuinely steeper (inflation becoming more responsive to unemployment when U<U\*), changes in inflation expectations, supply-side noise, and changes in $\mathrm { U ^ { * } }$ . The Phillips Curve data do seem to demonstrate a steeper slope lately, in the extremely low unemployment segment near 3.5% (Figure 4). Our favoured explanation is that $\mathrm { U ^ { * } }$ is low but hasn<sup>’</sup>t changed, so this localized steepening below 4% unemployment probably reflects expectations which are fairly anchored, but also a bit backward-looking, responding partially to the supply-side shocks and recovery of recent years. This steeper slope also might not be intrinsic to sub-4% unemployment itself, but to the features of the pandemic that delivered it. Large fiscal support/saving rate swings, lockdowns and reopening arguably couldn<sup>’</sup>t be fully summarized by the unemployment rate once labour supply hit a saturation point, leading to greater inflation and making the Phillips Curve appear steeper.

## Beveridge returning to room temperature

Direct evidence from the labour market corroborates the view that U\* is relatively low and stable. We have covered previously (links here and here) the administrative features which have dampened the peaks in wage inflation, mitigating risks of an inflationary spiral. The slow-moving wage mechanism is probably playing an additional role in keeping inflation expectations stable as well.

Figure 5: Beveridge curve (unemployment at t, vacancies at t-1) Vacancies\*100/Labour force
![](images/1e359d517899437cb23bd5eae718a0b6384bf1bb9d57b7447dcd4495ad98608e.jpg)

It was always possible that a very tight labour market and demand/supply changes induced since the pandemic could create structural mismatches in the labour market. This would raise NAIRU over time. However, as the labour market has cooled, the heat has disproportionately come out of vacancies, as opposed to employment. The Beveridge curve (Figure 5) is shifting back in, which suggests risk of a persistent skills mismatch, and rising NAIRU are not playing out so far.

## What makes a man turn neutral

Taylor Rule estimates shift one for one with assumptions on the neutral rate. Above, we have plugged in a real neutral rate of 0.4% (nominal neutral of 2.9%). The neutral rate could of course be higher (or lower), and the RBA previously outlined a suite of in-house models which range from negative, to +2% in real terms. Senior leadership haven<sup>’</sup>t spoken much on the issue, but generally focused on a nominal neutral estimate of 3.5%. Even putting this into the Taylor Rule, the latter would converge to the actual cash rate at 4Q24.

We have regarded fiscal policy as lending upside risks to the cash rate. The terms of trade remain very high, and related company tax receipts have pushed the Commonwealth Budget back to surplus. Fiscal space has opened up and is starting to leak back to households via tax cuts, landing this year. There is scope for more of this to follow, which would raise households<sup>’</sup> interest-servicing capacity. To the extent that government spending programs or tax policy changes boost incentives to work (e.g. participation) and raise productive capacity in other ways, they raise estimates of potential growth and so, the neutral rate. If they only add to demand, however, they will represent another shock which lowers the unemployment rate, possibly pushing the economy back onto a steeper segment of the Phillips Curve and requiring tighter policy for cyclical reasons.

Figure 6: Labour compensation per hour, and hours worked %oya
![](images/297f5f417adee311709bf50b721bc59fb36a267ea22847894760fcdb534d3d52.jpg)

Either way, a clear reacceleration in GDP seems required to make the case for higher rates. And to attribute this to a higher neutral rate would also mean abandoning concerns about weak productivity, which policy hawks have been so focused on. Hours and productivity have been inversely related during this cycle, as have hours, and earnings per hour (Figure 6). This degree of symmetry is conspicuous and suggests measurement noise in hours (see here and here), and changes in relative industry performance during lockdown/reopening have distorted the productivity picture. We expect GDP growth to remain bounded at about 2% this year, while productivity growth improves, shaking off noise from previous reopening effects.

Were the economy to reaccelerate more noticeably, productivity dynamics will likely guide perceptions of whether this is a demand-driven story which requires more restrictive policy, flatter yield curves etc., or one which may be consistent with a higher neutral rate.

## United States

• January CPI was a brusque reminder that the path back to 2% inflation will have bumps on the way

The trend in retail sales growth looks to be moderating after a strong 2023

• Disappointing prints on January manufacturing output and housing starts

• Fed minutes next week could shed light on QT plans

The story of 2023 was one of downside surprises on inflation and upside surprises on growth. This week<sup>’</sup>s news about the start of 2024 flipped that around: the January CPI came in hot while retail sales stumbled out of the gate. A broad-based firming in prices supported a 0.4% increase in the ex-food and energy core CPI, the biggest gain since last April. Additional data from the PPI suggest that the Fed<sup>’</sup>s preferred core PCE metric will also show a firm increase. Meanwhile retail sales fell 0.8% last month, indicating that real consumer spending likely modestly declined last month.

For both inflation and consumer spending, one swallow does not a summer make (particularly with January data). The ugly inflation number came after a reminder that broader inflationary psychology remains in check, as the NY Fed<sup>’</sup>s 3-year ahead inflation expectations measure released this week dropped to a low for the series that began in 2013 (February moves in the University of Michigan survey inflation expectations were pretty minor). Meanwhile, the still-low level of jobless claims reported this week is an indicator that the most important support for consumer spending—labor income—is likely continuing to grow.

The tone from Fed speakers this week continued to echo speeches from last week indicating an intent to eventually ease, but also not rush into the first rate cut. So far, the January data do not appear to be having a big impact on their rhetoric. We continue to look for the first cut of the cycle in June. While this week<sup>’</sup>s CPI report has clearly hawkish implications, it<sup>’</sup>s worth recalling that there are effectively three more CPI reports before the June meeting, which is plenty of time for the disinflation to get back on track. Perhaps more importantly, there are four more employment reports between now and then, which is plenty of time for boomy job growth to get derailed.

Fed chair Powell already has indicated that there was some discussion about plans for the balance sheet at the January meeting. The minutes to that meeting (released on Wednesday) could give us more insight into these plans, and we continue to expect a more formal announcement to come following the March meeting. Apart from the minutes, we don<sup>’</sup>t

expect major news coming from the economic reports in the upcoming holiday-shortened workweek.

## The undead CPI report

The January CPI data were firmer than expectations, with the headline up 0.3% during the month and the core index rising 0.4% (0.392% to three decimals). The broad trend in headline inflation continued to moderate, with the related year-ago rate cooling from 3.4% to 3.1%oya between December and January. The year-ago rate on the core also continued to trend lower into January, but it is hard to feel confident that the broad trend for core inflation is moderating when the January increase was the largest monthly gain since April 2023 and the related three-month run rate picked up to its highest level since June 2023, hitting 4.0% saar in January (Figure 1). While the January strength in the core CPI marked a change from what had been a softer trend over the prior few months, that month on its own may not be indicative of the new underlying trend.

Figure 1: Core CPI run rates
![](images/2eef483b2ec54e7f5cecd40979bcbf9d83c66067990e35e0965595bd19778415.jpg)

There were only a few major categories of the CPI with weak readings in January. Energy prices fell 0.9% in January, continuing their recent downward trend. Apparel prices also continued their recent soft run, declining 0.7% in January. Used vehicle prices, meanwhile, dropped 3.4% that month, mark ing their largest monthly decline since 1969. This plunge in used vehicle prices kept the broad core goods aggregate trending lower in January (-0.3%), but away from used vehicles, core goods prices ticked up 0.1% in January, marking their largest monthly increase since early 2023. The food price index in the CPI, meanwhile, rose 0.4% in January, which was the largest increase since January 2023.

And the core services price index jumped 0.7% in January, posting the largest monthly increase since September 2022 and showing strong readings throughout many of the related subcategories. The supercore index (core services ex. rent measures) jumped 0.8% in January, marking one of its largest monthly increases in recent decades. And owners<sup>’</sup> equivalent rent also looked firm, rising 0.6% (although the tenants<sup>’</sup> rent

measure looked much softer).

We also saw firmness in the other major January inflation reports, with upside surprises in the PPI and import price data for the month. Factoring in the related relevant data in hand, we look for strong January increases in the PCE price data. We estimate that the headline PCE price index rose 0.4% in January (2.4%oya) while the core PCE deflator increased 0.47% (2.9%oya). As mentioned above, the strength in inflation in January does not appear to have meaningfully pushed consumer inflation expectations higher yet.

## A slow start for retail spending

The January retail sales data were weak, and there were also downward revisions to some earlier relevant figures. Overall, the news was a significant disappointment relative to the consensus expectation and a more modest downside surprise relative to our own forecast. Before the report, we had argued that the seasonal factors would be “harsh<sup>”</sup> for this January and that these would generate weakness in the seasonally adjusted figures for the month and also that we should look across some of the recent volatility in the data (December was strong) to get a better sense of the underlying trend.

Figure 2: Nominal retail sales
![](images/c025d3b92ac196dd471e4329ca9ac52e66aa3939576a30410d719757b7553cc2.jpg)

Total retail sales dropped 0.8% in January (Figure 2), and sales of the important control group declined 0.4%. There were also downward revisions for the control group for earlier months, although December still looked solid, with a 0.6% gain that month (down from 0.8% previously reported). With the available data in hand, we think that real consumer spending declined about 0.2% in January (a weather-related surge in demand for heating consumption prevented a weaker outcome). Through some of the monthly ups and downs, it appears that the trend in consumer spending is moderating, and we think that real spending is on track for an increase of around 2% saar in 1Q.

## Other activity measures weak as well

There was some other disappointing news for January this week with respect to manufacturing production and housing starts and permits. The harsh winter weather may have been at least partly to blame here, with the Fed<sup>’</sup>s IP press release specifically noting weather as a drag on manufacturing (and mining) output in January and housing construction indicators often influenced by weather as well. Manufacturing output dropped 0.5% in January, and while we might want to discount the signal from this decline on its own, the broader trend has looked weak lately (particularly when excluding motor vehicles and parts for which production has been influenced by strike-related issues in recent months).

Meanwhile, housing starts fell 14.8% to 1.331mn saar in January and permits declined 1.5% to 1.470mn saar. The relatively good news in the report was that single-family permits—an important leading indicator—continued to trend higher, rising 1.6% in January. But most of the other major details were weak, including a 4.7% drop in single-family starts and even large declines in the multifamily data (starts: -35.6%, permits: -7.9%). The multifamily data are often very noisy from month to month, but the broad trends for these measures have been weakening lately while the single-family data generally have been trending higher (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Housing permits
![](images/c7c4d1755ca30381c32136ec083b4e370daf05cd1364227ed3e9dfc6b8f5d293.jpg)

But some other recent reports give us confidence that the weak January prints discussed above are not indicative of a clear downward break for the economy. Monthly indicators can often be noisy and the special issues associated with seasonal factors and winter weather may make the recent figures particularly distorted relative to the underlying trends. Moreover, jobless claims filings have continued to come in at low levels into February, suggesting that the labor market has remained strong lately. The few business surveys that have released February data so far (covering manufacturing, services, and housing specifically) also point to improvement that month relative to January. While the link between these surveys and the “hard<sup>”</sup> economic data is not particularly strong, the firming in the surveys does not point to intensifying weakness.

The January consumer price index (CPI) data were firmer than expectations, with the headline up 0.3% during the month and the core index rising 0.4% (0.392% to three decimals). The broad trend in headline inflation continued to moderate, with the related year-ago rate cooling from 3.4% to 3.1% oya between December and January. The year-ago rate on the core also continued to trend lower into January, although with rounding it came in at 3.9% oya in both December and January. But it is hard to feel confident that the broad trend for core inflation is moderating when the January increase was the largest monthly gain since April 2023 and the related three-month run rate picked up to its highest level since June 2023, hitting 4.0% saar in January. While the January strength in the core CPI marked a change from what had been a softer trend over the prior few months, that month on its own may not be indicative of the new underlying trend.

There were only a few major categories of the CPI with weak readings in January. Energy prices fell 0.9% in January, continuing their recent downward trend. Apparel prices also continued their recent soft run, declining 0.7% in January. Used vehicle prices, meanwhile, dropped 3.4% that month, marking their largest monthly decline since 1969. This plunge in used vehicle prices kept the broad core goods aggregate trending lower in January (-0.3%), but away from used vehicles, core goods prices ticked up 0.1% in January, marking their largest monthly increase since early-2023. The food price index in the CPI, meanwhile, rose 0.4% in January, which was the largest increase since January 2023. And the core services price index jumped 0.7% in January, posting the largest monthly increase since September 2022 and showing strong readings throughout many of the related subcategories. The supercore index (core services ex. rent measures) jumped 0.8% in January, marking one of its largest monthly increases in recent decades. And owners<sup>’</sup> equivalent rent also looked firm, rising 0.6% (although the tenants<sup>’</sup> rent measure looked much softer, moving up “only<sup>”</sup> 0.3% in January)

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv.

The January retail sales data were weak, and there were also downward revisions to some earlier relevant figures. Overall, the news was a significant disappointment relative to the consensus expectation and a more modest downside surprise relative to our own forecast. Before the report, we had argued that the seasonal factors would be “harsh<sup>”</sup> for this January and that these would generate weakness in the seasonally adjusted figures for the month and also that we should look across some of the recent volatility in the data (December was strong) to get a better sense of the underlying trend.

Getting into the numbers, total retail sales dropped 0.8% in January, and sales of the important control group declined 0.4%. There were also downward revisions for the control group for earlier months, although December still looked solid, with a 0.6% gain that month (down from 0.8% previously reported). Through some of the monthly ups and downs, it appears that the trend in consumer spending is moderating, and we think that real spending is on track for an increase of around 2% saar in 1Q (obviously very early in the quarter with respect to tracking). This is a few tenths below our expectations for the quarter heading into the retail sales report.

The January import price data were much stronger than expectations, with the headline import price index jumping 0.8%, and the nonfuel price measure up 0.7%. There were increases across many of the main categories in the report, and it is certainly possible that recent shipping issues have contributed to the January strength. That said, there was a 4.1% surge in imported prices for medicinal, dental, and pharmaceutical preparatory materials in January and away from this one category the import price data looked much less boomy during the month (there is not a very strong relationship between this specific import price index and the related CPI and PCE price measures). Overall, we think the latest news on import prices is consistent with the idea that the recent weakness in (core) goods inflation will end, but it is not totally obvious that it implies a surge in domestic prices to come.

Industrial production declined 0.1% in January, matching our forecast but disappointing the consensus. There was a 0.5% drop in manufacturing output that month that disappointed expectations. Mining output was also weak, falling 2.3% in January, and utilities output, meanwhile, jumped 6.0% that month. The Fed's press release noted winter weather contributing to the weakness in manufacturing and mining and also the jump in utilities, so we should discount the signal from the latest monthly moves to at least a degree. But the January data did extend what has been a soft run for the manufacturing data lately, particularly when motor vehicles and parts are excluded.

The January producer price index (PPI) was above expectations and continued what has been a strong week for different January inflation reports. The headline index rose 0.3% that month and the core index (ex. food and energy) jumped 0.5%. This was one of the strongest monthly gains seen in the core over the past couple of years and this strength came after what had appeared to be an earlier moderating trend. The details of the PPI that factor into our PCE price tracking looked strong on net, and with the January CPI and PPI data now in hand we think that core PCE inflation is tracking 0.47% for that month (2.9% oya).

The University of Michigan consumer sentiment index rose 0.6-pt to 79.6 in the preliminary February report. This increase was a little short of expectations but continued what has been an upward trend for sentiment in recent months. The survey<sup>’</sup>s inflation expectation measures were fairly uneventful in the preliminary February report in what has generally been a strong week for inflation data. The median one-year-ahead inflation expectation rose 0.1%-pt to 3.0% in February, undoing just a small portion of the 1.6%-pt drop over the prior two months, and the median five-year-year inflation expectation held at 2.9% between January and February.

# Focus: January inflation changes aren’t always oneoffs

The upside surprise in the January CPI report—headline 0.3%, core 0.4%—has led many to ask if the strong January data should be viewed as sign of an upshift in the inflation trend or if it likely reflects one-off price adjustments that kicked in at the start of the year. We think the truth may be somewhere between these two ideas—there were some jumps in the January data that look like they could be one-time price adjustments but we also don<sup>’</sup>t see systematic evidence that January inflation prints are worse than changes for other months in terms of signaling future core CPI inflation. We therefore think that core CPI inflation rates will step down in upcoming months relative to what we saw in January, but we don<sup>’</sup>t expect particularly soft readings in upcoming reports given the strength in the recent data<sup>2</sup>. There is some evidence, however, that January core PCE inflation changes are less useful than changes from other months in terms of signaling future changes in this inflation measure.

Core services provided much of the strength in the January CPI data. Within core services, it does appear that some of the January price increases reflected discreet one-time changes. Postage prices, for example, jumped 1.4% in January, reflecting price increases implemented by the Postal Service on January 21 (Figure 1). Prices for water sewerage and maintenance also surged 1.2% in January (Figure 1). While not as obviously tied to a single nationwide price change, some of the largest increases in prices for this category have occurred in recent Januarys.

Figure 1: Select categories of core services CPI inflation %m/m, sa, both scales
![](images/bc76da5c36a603ad87fa8a4a9cd04ba0767f3002a2ebd841b873f979f2c86c1c.jpg)

Given the changes discussed above, we examine more systematically the signal we usually get from Januarys with respect to future inflation changes. If annual price adjustments are implemented in Januarys for many types of goods and services, perhaps January is not a useful month in terms of signaling broader price trends. To test this, we look at how monthly inflation changes predict future inflation changes over 1-month, 3-month, and 12-month horizons<sup>3</sup>. Core CPI changes generally give some signal about future inflation changes, with the signals in January pretty much in line with the average across all months of the year (Figure 2). For the core services CPI, the signals in January are somewhat more reliable than those from other months on average (not shown). On the whole, these results suggest that we should not automatically discount the signal from January CPI changes just because they might reflect changes implemented at the start of the year.

Figure 2: Core CPI signal for future changes by month Coefficient
![](images/9a093f05c59a8c91a8b5ea84e6b70d5d92d7f1cf07d8ef00bc9c8dcdd4e04a26.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan. Note: Coefficient represents relationship between monthly change in specified month and average monthly changes in specified subsequent months.

Shifting to PCE prices, we do find evidence that Januarys generally are less reliable than other months in terms of signaling future inflation changes (Figure 3). So it does appear that January PCE price data could incorporate more one-time adjustments that are not reflective of underlying inflation trends than January CPI data.

Figure 3: Core PCE signal for future changes by month Coefficient
![](images/8392b6e626cc2a97647a48fac92e7348fbe837ec5edcaca7fde2a49b3ee51da9.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan. Note: Coefficient represents relationship between monthly change in specified month and average monthly changes in specified subsequent months.

## Euro Area

• Stark contrast between weak GDP and strong employment growth

• Fall in productivity suggests pressure on corporates, but strong pricing acts as an offset

• Surge in IP is due to Ireland

• Next week: PMI to improve a bit further, final HICP details to clarify recent momentum

This week was relatively light on data, but one striking aspect was the continued contrast between weak GDP growth and strong employment growth. This had already been implied by the monthly unemployment reports, but was made very explicit by this week<sup>’</sup>s second estimate of 4Q23 GDP, which included an estimate of employment. While GDP was left largely unrevised at 0.2%q/q saar, employment grew by a strong 1.3%q/q saar. This implies a further decline in output per person, continuing the trend of recent quarters. At the same time, country-level information continues to point to a strong increase in the GDP deflator, which limits the pressure this is putting on corporates. Overall, signals are still mixed about corporates in 4Q23, with capex looking to have declined while hiring increased further.

Against this backdrop, this week<sup>’</sup>s ECB commentary has remained cautious about the timing of a first rate cut. We remain comfortable with our June call, but this remains highly data dependent. Next week<sup>’</sup>s flash PMI for February and the final HICP details for January will be key to watch. The PMI has improved since October, but needs to improve by another 1pt to validate our 1Q24 GDP forecast of 0.5%q/q saar. Similarly, the flash HICP report implied a pick-up in the core inflation momentum as prior distortions faded, but, the ECB has yet to publish its estimate of seasonally-adjusted services prices. This will be important to see, together with the contribution made by the various tax and policy changes that affected the data in January.

## Solid hiring defies real GDP weakness

The second estimate of 4Q23 GDP saw growth revised up marginally to 0.2%q/q saar. No revisions were made to prior quarters, although an aggregation of country data suggest that 3Q23 could get revised up 0.25%-pt to -0.25%q/q saar when the third estimate is published. Looking at the last four quarters, growth averaged 0.2%q/q saar on the national data and 0.4%-q/q saar on an ex-Ireland basis (Figure 1). This is clearly not strong and fell short of the surveys in the middle of 2023, but it is nevertheless modestly positive. Revisions to prior quarters will be made in the third estimate, which will also give the expenditure and production details. Country details hint at a possible modest increase in consumer spending and a sharp fall in capex, only some of which may relate to weather effects on construction.

![](images/9cc4e8e931dd6bc327db0a505fa5f5370be945329ce688646eaa8973834ab7a6.jpg)

While the indications of weaker capex in 4Q23 send a cautionary note about corporates, the strong 1.3%q/q saar gain in employment qualifies that message. It is possible that this increase was skewed towards Spain and the public sector, but partial data also show significant increases outside of Spain and the PMI employment index suggests hiring by corporates as well (Figure 2). Overall, employment growth has averaged 1.2% ar over the past year, which is remarkable given the weaker trend in GDP.

Figure 2: Euro area employment
%q/q saar, model estimated over pre-2008 period
![](images/35cb44057e7d58df1f6c1d704a4b305faaa6a90ac94bce1721b5ad6f2f27861e.jpg)

Figure 3: Euro area labour productivity
![](images/31f1c4cb0b2b981a4964aaba77edca52904c2cf6f3b8151a968b5577e8754d2e.jpg)

The implication of the relative strength of employment to GDP is that output per worker has fallen 1.1% oya in 4Q23, with a significant fall also during the quarter (Figure 3). We do not yet have data on hours worked, but the recent trend in output per hour has also been weak. We would not rule out upward revisions to real GDP rebalancing this over time, but high levels of reported labour shortages are likely making firms keen to hire as well. One factor that has buffeted the impact on profits is that the strong GDP deflator suggests a solid cost pass-through. National data so far are showing close to double-digit gains in 4Q23 in Germany, Spain and Austria, while France and the Netherlands are not far below 5%ar.

## An IP surge, entirely due to Ireland

The surge in Euro area IP in December mostly reflected a 23.5%m/m jump in the notoriously distorted Irish data. This left Euro area IP flat in 4Q23, but, on an ex-Ireland basis, IP rose 0.5%m/m in December and was still down around 2.5%q/q saar in 4Q23. The distortion was even more pronounced in manufacturing, which rose 7.6%m/m in December and was up 8.4%q/q saar in 4Q23 including Ireland, while the ex-Ireland data were +0.3%m/m in December and -3.4%q/q saar in 4Q23 (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Euro area IP - manufacturing
![](images/aaa2271750745952d8b572facfee5b7b32541303e2d03af996711a41fb774a1b.jpg)

The output index of the Euro area manufacturing PMI has improved noticeably in recent months, but its 4pt increase to 46.5 is still signalling an underlying pace of contraction. Hence, that manufacturing output in the Euro area ex-Ireland contracted in 4Q23 is broadly consistent with the surveys. At the country level, the ex-Ireland weakness remains highly concentrated in Germany, but, as illustrated by the more detailed national report, .there are some aspects of the German weakness that are puzzling. In particular, the contrast between high order backlogs and weak output remains odd, as does the gap between factory sales and output and the gap between IP and GVA in manufacturing. Next week<sup>’</sup>s PMI will provide an important update on this.

Figure 5: Euro area manufacturing output and PMI
![](images/65ce2e24e8caf6092469c70df0fdff9ef7649dde168d8e705e51d5d2d226a92c.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, Markit, J.P. Morgan

## The ECB’s new Wage Tracker

Last week, the ECB published a paper outlining its new Wage Tracker. This had been published in many ECB speeches, but the methodology was never entirely clear. In effect, the ECB has tried to replicate the official national data that feed into its quarterly Indicator of Negotiated Pay, using the same granular information on individual pay settlements. At present, the coverage of the Wage Tracker is more limited and there are also some curious methodological choices. For example, oneoff payments are smoothed and it is assumed that no pay increases occurred in the twelve months before a new deal. The latter choice was made to simplify the calculations. Due to these assumptions and the more limited coverage, there are some gaps between the national Wage Trackers and national data on negotiated pay, but, these have tended to cancel out at the Euro area level.

One benefit of the Wage Tracker is that it can show the forward implications of existing deals. In Figure 6, the Wage Tracker remains at around 4.5%oya throughout 2024, reflecting a rotation from larger one-off payments to more regular pay increases. Of course, the Wage Tracker cannot anticipate the new deals that are being negotiated during 1Q24 and therefore these are important to watch, but, what it does suggest is that the forward momentum in existing deals is still solid.

Figure 6: Euro area wage growth
![](images/d486baa4164e8b9e1a3abacb80083f43aa5c0dfa8439d8f69ce7a0117a17274a.jpg)

Both the German composite PMI and IFO worsened modestly in January. However, details of the manufacturing PMI showed further improvement from very low levels and hence with further scope for improvement. Also, the decline in January may not have broken the prior upward trend. At the level of GDP, the PMI was still signalling a 0.4% ar contraction in January, while IFO expectations were still much weaker (but has tracked GDP less well over the past two years). In our view, the underlying performance of the German economy is likely improving and therefore we have pencilled in an increase for the surveys. One uncertainty comes from the Red Sea disruptions, which could have an impact on the February surveys.

Euro area consumer confidence unexpectedly fell 1pt in January, breaking the prior upward trend. Nevertheless, views about the labour market remained solid, but these do not flow into the headline confidence measure. In addition, we suspect that perceptions of inflation are continuing to decline, given the much larger fall already seen in actual inflation. From a real incomes perspective, the constellation of solid nominal wage growth and a large fall in headline inflation is creating a supportive backdrop for households. We therefore expect confidence to move higher again.

The Euro area composite PMI improved further in January, although with mixed country details. The periphery has driven the recent improvement, with Germany improving in a more choppy way and France lagging behind. Disruptions in the Red Sea could impact the PMI in February after little visible impact in January, but, certain details from January point to a further improvement. In particular, the indices for future output and new orders had improved noticeably, while the employment index pointed to further hiring. At the country level, the German manufacturing details pointed to further lift and France could increase from very low levels. From the perspective of our GDP forecast, the PMI signalled growth at only a 0.15% ar. As a result, our 1Q24 forecast requires the

PMI to increase by another point. We have pencilled in a part of this for February.

We expect the final report to confirm the 1.1%q/q saar contraction in GDP reported in the flash report. No details had been published at the time of the flash, except for a very strong gain in the GDP deflator and some guidance that the GDP decline reflected weakness in construction and equipment investment. This suggests that consumer spending changed only modestly. The final report will reveal all of these details, including those behind an upwardly revised 3Q.

## Inflation

The final HICP details will be very important for assessing the underlying momentum in the data. First, weights distortions had pushed the reported momentum downwards in 4Q23. Second, January is particular uncertain due to new expenditure weights and delays in the publication of some country details. Due to the latter, the ECB did not publish its seasonally adjusted data for services. The implication of the flash report was that both core goods and services prices rose noticeably in seasonally adjusted terms, but it will be important to see confirmation of this in the final report. It will then also be easier to assess the impact of the various tax/policy changes affecting the data. Our expectation is that core momentum was solid in January, suggesting sticking in services inflation and a second consecutive gain in core goods prices.

## Review of past week’s data

Output and surveys

Real GDP

<table><tr><td></td><td>3Q23</td><td>Flash 4Q23</td><td>2nd Est. 4Q23</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area (second estimate)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%q/q, sa %q/q, saar</td><td>-0.1 -0.5</td><td>0.0 0.1</td><td>0.0 甲 0.2</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.1</td></tr></table>

The second estimate saw GDP marginally revised up 4Q23 by 0.1%-pt annualised. No revisions were made to prior quarters, although an aggregation of country data suggests that 3Q23 could get revised up by 0.25%-pt to -0.25%q/q saar when the third estimate is published. Looking at the last four quarters, growth averaged 0.2%q/q saar on the national data and 0.4%-q/q saar on an ex-Ireland basis. This is clearly not strong and fell short of the surveys in the middle of 2023, but it is nevertheless modestly positive. Revisions to prior quarters will be made in the third estimate, which will also give the expenditure and production details. Country details hint at possible modest increase in consumer spending and a sharp fall in capex, only some of which may relate to weather effects on construction.

## Industrial production

<table><tr><td></td><td>Oct</td><td></td><td>Nov</td><td>Dec</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Ind production (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.7</td><td></td><td>Q3</td><td>0.4</td><td>03</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td>%oya, sa Manuf prod (%m/m, sa)</td><td>5.7 -0.7</td><td>-5.8</td><td>64 0.2</td><td>-5.2 0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>1.0 7.6</td></tr></table>

The jump in Euro area IP largely reflected a 23.5%m/m jump in the notoriously distorted Irish data. This left total IP flat in

4Q23. On an ex-Ireland basis, IP rose 0.5%m/m in December and was still down around 2.5%q/q saar in 4Q23. The distortion was even more pronounced in manufacturing, which rose 7.6%m/m in December and was up 8.4%q/q saar in 4Q23 including Ireland, while the ex-Ireland data were +0.3%m/m in December and -3.4%q/q saar in 4Q23. The output index of the Euro area manufacturing PMI has improved noticeably, but has not yet clawed its way back into positive territory. Hence, an ex-Ireland contraction in 4Q23 is still broadly consistent with the surveys. At the country level, the ex-Ireland weakness was however highly concentrated in Germany.

## Demand and labour markets

<table><tr><td>Employment</td><td>2Q23</td><td>3Q23</td><td>4Q23</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Euro area (flash)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Sa</td><td>229</td><td>403</td><td></td><td>533</td></tr><tr><td>000s, diff. q/q</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>02</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>%q/q %oya</td><td>1.4</td><td>1.3</td><td>1</td><td>1.2</td></tr></table>

While the indications of weaker capex in 4Q23 send a cautionary note about corporates, the strong 1.3%q/q saar gain in employment qualifies that message. It is possible that this increase was skewed towards Spain and the public sector, but partial data also show significant increases outside of Spain and the PMI employment index suggests hiring by corporates as well. Overall, employment growth has averaged 1.2% ar over the past year, which is remarkable given the weaker trend in GDP. As a result, output per worker has fallen 1.1% oya, with a significant fall also in 4Q23. We do not yet have data on hours worked, but the recent trend in output per hour has also been weak. We would not rule out upward revisions to real GDP rebalancing this over time, but high levels of reported labour shortages are likely making firms keen to hire as well. One factor that has buffeted the impact on profits is that the strong GDP deflator suggests a solid cost passthrough.

## Japan

• 4Q GDP a washout, as expected

• Renewed yen depreciation underscores delicate balance for monetary policy

Sticking with our patient BoJ view: mid-year NIRP removal to be preceded by balance sheet adjustments

• Next week: Feb surveys edge up

This week<sup>’</sup>s 4Q GDP report was the star of the show for all the wrong reasons, with the economy shrinking 0.4%q/q saar and domestic demand sliding for a third consecutive quarter. Details confirmed that activity through year-end was a washout. That the consensus had been looking for a robust sequential gain in headline GDP had been puzzling—monthly data had long suggested that activity hit an air pocket last quarter, and we had expected a broadly flat outcome.

That Japan<sup>’</sup>s economy slipped into technical recession last quarter should put paid to the idea of a rapidly moving BoJ. We continue to expect gradualism, and patience. We remain of the view that NIRP removal will take place against the backdrop of a recovery in domestic demand, likely around mid-year after incomes receive a boost from robust Spring wage negotiations. But we expect BoJ to remain on the move. We look for the Bank to start adjusting forward guidance and/ or asset purchases at upcoming meetings. A tweak to (or de facto removal of) its ETF purchase program is probably lowhanging fruit at this point: with reflationary tailwinds already leaving domestic stock indices around post-bubble highs, this is something we have long viewed as being in the cards.

That said, it would be a mistake to interpret this week<sup>’</sup>s soft GDP report as any broader signal of a sharper sink into recession. Sentiment and labor market data have remained robust in the face of a normalization in activity. Last week<sup>’</sup>s December employment survey signalled still-robust hiring, and domestic-oriented confidence surveys remain elevated from a long-run perspective.

Figure 1: Japan real private consumption
2019=100, sa. Goods is wtd. avg. of durables and semi-durables
![](images/dbafac6be04840c4b18c619e42d21d5374e0e76f53dc31f82ac1a33a92f9863a.jpg)
Source: Cabinet Office, J.P. Morgan

It is also important to place the past several quarters<sup>’</sup> slip in business and consumer spending in the context of the prior reopening-fueled run-up in activity. Even after this week<sup>’</sup>s soggy GDP data, household demand for durable goods remains well above its pre-pandemic level, even as non-durable spending has contracted (Figure 1). Household spending on services remains softer, though demand-side GDP data understates the overall trend given that tourism-related spending is separated into services exports; these were also robust last quarter, bolstered by a weak yen that is re-testing postbubble lows on a trade-weighted basis. Indeed, that USD/JPY is again approaching levels that had sparked FX intervention highlights the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s delicate balancing act. We expect the Bank to walk a fine line between remaining cautious, but nonetheless signalling that it remains on the move so as to prevent fueling sharper yen depreciation.

We expect surveys next week to confirm that activity is not decelerating further, with the February services PMI likely to remain around recent elevated levels, and the Reuters Tankan survey to show some stabilization in manufacturers<sup>’</sup> outlooks. The exception here will be the auto sector, which is being depressed by scheduled plant shutdowns.

## 4Q GDP slips and slides, as expected

The Cabinet Office<sup>’</sup>s first estimate of 4Q GDP marked a second consecutive quarterly decline, with a 0.4%q/q, saar fall. This was even weaker than our below-consensus expectations (JPM: 0%, Consensus: 1.1%). 3Q GDP was also revised down from -2.9% to -3.3%.

The main source of weakness remained domestic demand, and all domestic demand components marked quarterly declines in 4Q. Domestic demand has remained lackluster since 2Q23, contributing negatively to overall growth for three consecutive quarters. In 4Q, weak domestic demand was partly offset by solid net exports, while inventories contributed negatively to overall growth, by 0.3%-pt, on our estimate (Figures 2 and 3).

Figure 2: Japan Real GDP
![](images/96a969d67478816d0fc88cf10438c810c3d4366e5b6e163a193483a1302f9dc4.jpg)
Source: Cabinet Office, J.P. Morgan

Figure 3: Japan Contribution to real GDP growth %-pt contr. to q/q saar, 2qma
![](images/fe37e12d8d0ac7f245bfe3f569645c601a6869a617c2e486a4e1ee175d53ae1f.jpg)

## In the details:

Private consumption fell 0.9%q/q saar, marking the third consecutive quarterly decline. We had expected negative consumption growth in 4Q based on the monthly real consumption data, but the actual decline was larger than expected. The persistent real income drags have dampened consumption activity since 2Q. As consumer sentiment is recovering, we expect private consumption to recover in coming quarters, reflecting the recent rise in wage momentum, albeit at a modest pace (Figure 4).

Capex was weaker than we expected, with private equipment investment contracting 0.3%q/q saar, after a 2.4% drop in 3Q. Despite the strong business sentiment backed by favorable corporate revenue, investment activity had remained soft. Yet, we continue to expect fixed investment to trend higher, as the backdrop of ongoing labor shortages likely will catalyze a shift higher in firms<sup>’</sup> capital expenditure (Figure 5).

Both exports and imports of goods and services posted strong gains, up 11.0%q/q saar and 7%, respectively (Figure 6).

We think the 4Q GDP print has important implications for the sequence of the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s policy normalization. The BoJ has lately begun to hint at rising confidence to start monetary policy normalization, and the majority of market participants currently view NIRP removal in the spring as a preset course.

In this context, at the January post-meeting press conference, Governor Ueda expressed his view that domestic demand was strong during the year-end and New Year holidays, but today<sup>’</sup>s report challenged that view. The Governor has also indicated that the Bank will “lift the NIRP when demand will be strong.<sup>”</sup> With domestic demand remaining lackluster, we continue to consider it difficult for the Bank to justify further curbing domestic demand by raising policy rates to tame inflation, which has been moderating somewhat recently.

While we expect the BoJ to start policy adjustments related to forward guidance and asset purchases at upcoming meetings, we continue to view NIRP removal as a mid-year event when domestic demand likely will recover on the back of strong wage growth in the spring.

## Canada

• We expect core CPI accelerated in January

• Annual price resets expected to be firm

• Bank of Canada will remain on hold in 1Q24

Next week the January CPI report will be released, the last inflation report the Bank of Canada will see before the March 6 monetary policy announcement. Our forecast is 0.2%sa, which translates to 3.3% on a year-over-year basis following 3.4%oya in December. Core CPI (excluding food and energy) is estimated to have increased 0.3%sa last month or an acceleration to 3.6%oya compared with 3.4%oya in December. But January tends to be a noisy month for reasons discussed below. With the core CPI expected be glimpsed unfavorably by the Bank of Canada, we place a low probability on any action at the March meeting. We judge that a sense of uncertainty around interpreting the January result will keep the Bank on hold hence the policy rate will close out 1Q24 at 5.0%. There will be three more CPI reports ahead of the June 5 announcement. We are sticking with our call that the BOC will start lowering rates at that time (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Headline CPI
![](images/655c6b3b0aa89af0344a229f5079c19c8a0300f35acc826eabd0e5a3f2a39b59.jpg)

January is a prime survey month for myriad CPI sub-categories that are only surveyed annually, semi-annually, or at the start of a quarter. It<sup>’</sup>s generally a noisy month of annual price resets with a jumble of annual tax, fee, and overlay adjustments by provinces and businesses. With this in mind we see upside risk to our forecast. We believe the recent jump in property tax costs (a category surveyed only once a year in October) hints at firmer annual changes in provincial fees. Property taxes and other special charges jumped 4.9%oya in October, the largest annual change since October 1992, as municipalities leaned in to catch-up to cover rising costs (Figure 2). We expect a notably firm increase in utility rates for water and some other sub-categories of owned accommodation that cover related fees. Higher costs related to new redtape regulations concerning remote workers and child care operators could show up in firmer prices for categories like household operations. Moreover, we expect 4Q23 provincial increases in the minimum wage will continue to reverberate in service categories like food purchased from restaurants. Energy costs are expected to be partially offsetting on headline CPI as lower gasoline prices are set to be a drag on a monthly and annual basis. Food prices should be firmer on a monthly basis (nsa) than recent months on seasonal influences but the year-on-year change should continue to trend lower. The seasonally adjusted values for the major categories will be revised for the last three years with the January CPI release. The revisions could have implications for the recent inflation trends and the outlook for the next few months.

Figure 2: CPI-Property taxes and other special charges
![](images/20ea2a4f592559040a62f67b4c7b25f5bbdbf6545c807fdb9b56f304a3208870.jpg)

## Rough quarter for the goods side

Real manufacturing output (sales and inventories) declined 6.0%saar in 4Q23, a notable slump in the pandemic recovery. The weakness was spread across durable and non-durable goods but the principal drag was a plunge in volumes of motor vehicle and parts manufacturing. Volume sales of manufactured motor vehicles and parts declined 37%ar following four consecutive quarterly increases while real exports of motor vehicles and parts fell 6.4%ar. The decline was pinned to various factors including a labor strike in the sector and a retooling slowdown of production in some auto assembly plants. We keep our forecast but the weakness suggests some downside risk to our 1.5%ar real GDP estimate for 4Q23. Aggregate manufacturing unfilled and new orders are pointing to a rebound (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Manufacturing production pipeline
![](images/f91b840b407f894d29d9af5b7d033694830b5b9ce1cbc6bb2186da83339768ef.jpg)

## Mexico

• Banxico minutes to elaborate on the right moment to cut

Inflation falling to 4.7% should make Banxico comfortable easing as soon as March

• GDP to speed up after running in low gear in 4Q23

• Next week<sup>’</sup>s stagnant 4Q23 GDP to contrast with survey data

At last week<sup>’</sup>s policy statement, Banxico opened the door for cuts by replacing “will maintain the current level of the policy rate for some time<sup>”</sup> with “will assess the possibility of adjusting the policy rate in the forthcoming meetings.<sup>”</sup> We expect the minutes will elaborate on the appropriate time to cut rates.

To us, the emphasis on “disinflation gains<sup>”</sup> and “passive tightening<sup>”</sup> in previous discussions suggests most members are convinced that the time for easing policy is at the next policy decision, in March. As two members mentioned in the previous minutes, the passive tightening has been considerable since the end of the hiking cycle (close to 100bp now) and while there are still some pockets of concern, core inflation has adjusted as expected with core goods surprising to the downside and some services delivering encouraging news as well. We elaborated on these dynamics in a recent note. True, inflation expectations remain slightly above long-term gauges but to us, the ongoing inflation corrections and the vigilant approach from Banxico should pave the way for further corrections in expectations soon.

At any rate, Banxico<sup>’</sup>s prudence should last longer and we expect the minutes will be clear about it, suggesting that even if a majority of members are open to a first cut as soon as March, the vigilant stance will keep all options open for the following meetings, at least in May and probably June. Most members have pronounced in favor of caution given non-linearities in price-formation and the potential spikes related to geopolitical events and weather conditions.

With non-core inflation pressures expected to abate as soon as this month (see next section) and core goods supported by a strong currency and limited damage from supply disruptions, we believe the board has a compelling argument to start reducing the tight stance, with the ex-ante policy rate looming around 7.5%. Part of the caution will of course be related to the potential tightening in relative monetary conditions versus the Fed and the implications for the currency, but this discussion will only gain traction once the Fed cuts approach, likely midyear.

Finally, we expect the board to elaborate some on recent growth disappointments, particularly after 4Q23 came in flat and capacity utilization for December—among other indicators—suggests economic activity is entering the late phase of expansion. As demand pressures abate, we anticipate Banxico will adjust its narrative in favor of a more neutral stance, which should allow it to ease consistently in 2H24 to 9.75% (Figure 1). But we expect the vigilant narrative to remain in place for a while, with a pause still likely in June.

Figure 1: Banxico easing cycles bps (end-of-cycle rate in parentheses); t +1 = month of first cut
![](images/26a7f4028e0a656d2530838e0dfa31925847d023160eff430353145cd2fae71e.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from Banxico and Haver. \*No forecasts beyond 2025

## 1H Feb CPI softer on non-core relief

Next week<sup>’</sup>s preliminary February CPI is likely to bring Banxico a step closer to cutting in March. It should confirm that the recent acceleration in headline inflation is being rapidly unwound, and that core has cooled, even if it remains a long stretch from target. We expect headline prices to have risen just 0.13%2w/2w, as a 0.27% gain in core prices is offset by a big drawdown in raw food prices. This would bring both headline and core inflation to 4.7%oya.

Our view is that cyclical forces will keep core inflation from moving decisively lower; in fact, we expect core price gains to accelerate again in February to 0.4%m/m, sa, based on the preliminary forecast. We expect gains to remain near those rates through 1H24, keeping 3m/3m annualized core inflation above 4%.

This should prove the case particularly on the back of sticky services inflation (we pencil in a 0.5%m/m, sa gain in Feb core services CPI), a function of a positive output gap and changing dynamics in wage policy and income distribution. But that does not negate the fact that inflation and core inflation have come down a long way from their peaks. We think, however, that were core inflation to follow our playbook (and not Banxico<sup>’</sup>s), the risk of a pause right after the first rate cut is not negligible (Figure 2).

![](images/2223540d1520152458cca0b1e03682e84f2dcda8be00253bfb828a04dc0a83f4.jpg)

## Dec GDP proxy to confirm 4Q stagnation

Next week we will get the second revision of last quarter<sup>’</sup>s GDP. We expect very modest changes in aggregate, with real GDP up 0.2%ar instead of 0.3% as per the flash reading. This would be consistent with the marginal December GDP data point at -0.1%m/m, sa. The question in our minds is what to make of the marked slowdown in activity.

Our view is it is transitory, and growth will reaccelerate in 1H24. The approach to getting us there is two-pronged: first, fundamentally, not a lot seems to have changed (consumers and businesses are on a strong footing); second, survey data have shown either resilience at high levels or a rapid turn-up in December/January, depending on the sector. We have not been expecting much, and continue not to, from manufacturing, but strong survey data did not have a counterpart in hard data for December in construction, which we had expected. We are holding to our view of strength in construction, but the December test (output data) of survey data failed.

We expect services GDP data to bring some balance: perceived demand for services surged in the Dec INEGI survey and we look for a 0.4%m/m, sa jump in the Dec services GDP proxy to be released next week (retail sales should also show a healthy 0.5% gain).

As said, at an aggregate level, this does little to change the weak growth outturn in 4Q, but would, by contrast, show much more resilience in services than was implied in the GDP flash release (our Dec forecast is consistent with 2.1%ar services growth in 4Q vs. 0.5% in the flash) and a much weaker industrial sector, which for the moment we largely attribute to a transitory downshift in construction<sup>’</sup>s upward trend. For what it is worth, our survey-based GDP proxy pointed to 2.9%3m3m annualized GDP growth through January (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Survey-based economic activity index and GDP proxy
![](images/353d9d5dfff8e9d2a5a7a8513a9b1446ad0157551abe376377800a5dbc31ddd1.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from INEGI. Dec GDP proxy implied in flash 4Q GDP

## Brazil

• Congress sessions to come back into the spotlight

• Agenda includes revenue measures and tax regulation

• Administration will have to focus to avoid pitfalls

• Incoming data may reinforce more benign scenario for activity

As usual, festivities surrounding the half-week-long Carnival helped quiet political noise, with most headlines focused on the challenges the government will face with Congress getting back to business or the impacts of recent police raids on allies of former president Bolsonaro. Attention should now shift to the agenda defended by Finance Minister Haddad, whose team bets on both recurring and non-recurring measures collecting 1.7% of GDP in revenues to deliver a balanced budget for the year (Table 1).

Table 1: Fiscal measures included in official budget
<table><tr><td></td><td>Budget</td><td>JPM</td></tr><tr><td>Legal disputes (tax litigation, CARF and others)</td><td>1.2</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Taxes on wealth</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Onshore closed funds and offshore structures</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>End of Interest on Capital/Equity (JCP)</td><td>0.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>E-bets concessions and taxes</td><td>0.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Transfer prices</td><td>-</td><td>&lt;0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Increase in federal taxes (diesel)</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Others</td><td>0.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total</td><td>1.7</td><td>0.8</td></tr></table>

Source: Finance Ministry, Eurasia Group, Media outlets and J.P.Morgan

The attempt to overturn the extension of payroll tax breaks and to end existing tax benefits to companies in the services sector, known as PERSE, is a key part of this agenda. Lawmakers pointed out the need to negotiate these matters separately, and the government may choose to do so by taking a more gradualist approach to the payroll discussion. While all these topics will likely take a while longer to be settled, being part of a provisionary measure that will not expire until 2Q24, we expect negotiations to begin in earnest next week.

Other themes that were punted by the government as priorities, and will be increasingly monitored, include the bills to regulate the new tax system and an income tax reform, as determined by the constitutional amendment approved in 2023. There is also potential for new legislation on environmental and administrative issues. With so much to cover, and municipal elections getting closer, we take this big wish list with a grain of salt, and anticipate the administration will have partial success in its bid to reinforce tax collection and refrain from supporting measures that work against it. In spite of Speaker Lira<sup>’</sup>s support for an administrative reform, the

topic is contentious. The distance between the proposal put forward by the previous government, which had fiscal savings as a guideline, and the current administration<sup>’</sup>s focus on improving human capital allocation is significant, and Senate President Pacheco has not shown any inclination to tackle the issue in the near term.

While investors wait to gain more clarity on these ebbs and flows, they will also monitor data that may influence the mood around Congress. January tax collection data will be released in coming weeks, and our BRL280bn forecast points to a noticeable acceleration in revenues (Figure 1). While not enough to drive expectations toward a balanced budget for the year, incoming results may prove sufficient to avoid a severe budget cut by mid-March, pushing discussion on a target change further into the future—we look for a change in the target to -0.5% between March and April. If that more optimistic outcome plays out, not getting all priorities approved would likely not be a problem after all.

## Argentina

Headline CPI came in a tad below expectations, decelerating to 20.6%m/m in January (254.2%oya)

• Food prices explained the bulk of the monthly print, amid FX pass-through from December’s devaluation

Core (+20.2%m/m) and ex. food core (+19.8%m/m) printed slightly below headline

We expect inflation to print in the double digits through 1Q23 on the need to correct relative prices

• December 2024 inflation is fine-tuned to 200%oya (290% annual average)

Headline CPI came in at 20.6%m/m in January (254.2%oya), fairly in line with our 21%m/m forecast. The monthly print decelerated when compared to the 25.5% reported in December (Figure 1), yet it remains the highest since March 1990, with over-year-ago inflation at levels similar to those reported at the beginning of the Convertibility Plan. This is explained by the carryover from the front-loaded realignment of the official FX in December. Indeed, we estimate the FX passthrough stood at around 39% in this first two months following the FX jump, in line with the historical average of past devaluation episodes, yet well below the 85% recorded in the first months after the August <sup>’</sup>23 FX adjustment. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan<sup>’</sup>s adjustment), national headline CPI gained 20.8%m/m, sa, with the last-three-month sequential pace skyrocketing to 784%saar.

Figure 1: Headline and core National-CPI
![](images/a1020da2bd46d938693e899180341bd943e009c90d131df37a21cda53ee381a0.jpg)

Regarding underlying CPI, both core and ex. food core printed a tad below headline inflation and our expectations. Core CPI decelerated further, to 20.2%m/m (a 14.8%-pt contribution), on the heels of a 28.3% increase the previous month. Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) also decelerated to 19.8%m/m. The seasonal adjustment shows core momentum still running at a very high 760% in annualized terms.

Food prices explained the bulk of the inflation print, amid a FX pass-through from the December devaluation and the expiration of price-freezing programs. In January, food inflation printed at 20.4%m/m (a 6.5%-pt contribution), in line with headline CPI. When zooming in on food prices, the increase was again broad-based, with two-digit increases in every category.

Regulated prices accelerated strongly on the month, while seasonal prices helped to contain inflation pressures. Regulated prices printed at 26.6%m/m (a 3.9%-pt contribution), a new record high in the series since 2016, despite the muted increases in energy tariffs. The increase was driven by fuel prices, up by 42.7%m/m, pre-paid medical services (+34.3%m/m), and public transport (outside the AMBA region). Worth noting, regulated prices will continue increasing in the coming months given the need to realign relative prices. Meanwhile, seasonal prices printed below headline at 16.2%m/m ( a 2%-pt contribution, Figure 2).

Figure 2: National-CPI
%-pt, contribution to headline CPI
![](images/6d3985656594c02b9850292a4a5ee8717a7d0f02f3b11ffca7fe49337d79888a.jpg)

Relative prices realignment will keep inflation in the double digits through March. We expect inflation to remain high in 1H24 amid the ongoing need to correct the sizable relative prices distortion promptly, a necessary condition for stabilization. The reduction of energy and transportation subsidies, that is, the increase in tariffs, will continue to add pressures on inflation throughout the year. Indeed, we expect the hike in transportation prices in the AMBA region would add around 2%-pts to the headline print in February, on top of the increases observed in the rest of the country this month. Adding to this is an increase in energy tariffs for high income consumers. Moreover, adjustments in electricity tariffs for low- and middle-income households, further correction in fuels amid higher gasoline taxes (likely effective in March) and increases in private services—communication, housing rents, education—are in the pipeline. Despite the lack of details on the timing and the amount of such corrections, in our assessment, repressed inflation still will contribute close to 15%-pts in the months ahead.

Against this backdrop, we expect inflation to print in the double digits through 1Q23. Our forecast entertains a discrete FX jump in June 2024 , after which we expect the economy to stabilize at single-digit inflation levels if the stabilization program proves successful. This path would be consistent with December 2024 inflation at 200%oya (290%oya average inflation). Admittedly, the risks to our 2024 forecast are skewed to the upside if the FX convergence is delayed or the fiscal adjustment faces implementation challenges.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Colombia: The adjustment is done, though weighed too much on capex

We revise full-year 2024 to 1.0%y/y, consistent with 2.0%4Q/4Q

• Peru: Activity ended 2023 on a poor footing, logging -0.6%m/m, sa

• We maintain 2024<sup>’</sup>s average annual GDP at +2.1%y/y, consistent with 2.6% 4Q/4Q

Cabinet changes should not significantly alter the fiscal path

## Colombia: The adjustment is done

The national accounts report showed that, in sequential terms, GDP gained by a tad in 4Q, +0.1%q/q, saar, on the heels of a 1.4% ar gain in 3Q23. The performance in 2H23 was actually stronger than in the first half of the year: GDP averaged just +0.1%ar in 1H23, but +0.8%ar in 2H23. That compares to average growth of +2.2%ar in 2022. In all, the revised series now shows a cyclical peak for GDP in 1Q23, with the 4Q23 GDP level down by 1.1% from the peak. The revisions proved more drastic for the nsa series: 2Q and 3Q were trimmed to +0.1%oya and -0.6%oya, from +0.4% and -0.3%, respectively. With 4Q recording +0.3%oya, the full-year GDP came at a poor +0.6%y/y, well below our and the BanRep Staff<sup>’</sup>s projections (see note).

Dissecting the demand components is useful to illustrate the macro-dynamics at play. First and foremost, domestic demand increased in 4Q, gaining 3.3%ar. But the quarterly expansion came after four consecutive quarterly declines. Indeed, by 4Q23 domestic demand printed 5.3% below the cyclical peak observed in 3Q22.

When breaking down domestic demand, gross capital formation gained 25%ar and consumption slipped again, by 0.6%ar. On the investment side, the bounce back should be read with a grain of salt, as fixed investment contracted again, for the fourth consecutive time (-15.3%ar), and closed the year 15.6% below its cyclical peak in 4Q22. On the other side, the consumption performance masks the gain in household consumption (+2.4%ar), favored by disinflation and good performance of the labor market, more than offset by government sliding 14.4%ar, to close the year 5.5% below the peak observed in 2Q. Trend-wise, consumption has proved stagnant since mid-2022.

Finally, net exports contributed negatively to GDP. Exports (in volumes) increased by 9.0%ar, but imports bounced back by a +23.4% annualized pace. A bright spot of the past six

quarters has been the good performance of exports, showing a steady expansion since 2020.

In all, despite the series revisions, the narrative on activity is unchanged. The economy has processed the required domestic demand correction after the overheating period in 2022. But the adjustment has proved suboptimal, with investment paying the lion<sup>’</sup>s share, which could compromise potential growth levels ahead. Consumption, on the other side, has landed softly at sustainable levels. As said, against this backdrop, exports<sup>’</sup> performance stands out. In all, in our view the output gap has already converged into negative territory, which is likely to be sustained through 2024, in line with the Staff<sup>’</sup>s base case (see note, Figure 1).

Figure 1: Colombia real domestic demand Lvl, 4Q19=100
![](images/20703eb69ab18e1e87db4249e36f54ba11352a09c1ecf2f60a9e1252df725acb.jpg)

When it comes to forecasts, following the revisions to prior quarters and the 4Q23 print, we revise full-year 2024 to 1.0%y/y, consistent with 2.0%4Q/4Q.

## Peru: Activity ended 2023 on a poor footing

When adjusting for seasonality, Peru<sup>’</sup>s activity index disappointed in December, logging -0.6 %m/m, sa. The sequential momentum slowed to 2.7%3m/3m, saar, which compares to -0.4% in 3Q23. While waiting for the quarterly national accounts, the activity index performance suggests the economy was able to grow a bit above its potential in the last quarter of the year, though December<sup>’</sup>s performance casts doubt on the ability to sustain a positive performance ahead. When measured on an over-year-ago basis, activity recorded -0.7%oya, with the annual print closing at -0.55%y/y (Figure 2).

The supply side breakdown showed manufacturing, fishing, and mining as the main culprits behind December<sup>’</sup>s monthly activity contraction. On the constructive side, the aggregated contribution of services was positive, though low at just +0.1%-pt. In 2024, we expect manufacturing, construction, and mining activity to firm up. Manufacturing and construction together subtracted 1.3%-pts from headline activity in 2023, while mining added 0.9%-pt.

Figure 2: Peru real activity
![](images/a808f7bfe6f4c217e67ec394b86b92db241e1c57c36e77f24fbeb33577ec5ba7.jpg)

All in, by December <sup>’</sup>23 headline activity printed just 2.5% above pre-pandemic levels as of February 2020. Translating the subpar aggregated growth figures into a per capita basis, growth in the last three years printed 2.0 % below the levels as of February 2020. Note that annual per capita growth was +4.4%, on average, in the period 2004-19.

Ahead, monetary easing should allow some sectors to regain positive momentum, such as construction and some services. Also, El Niño has so far proved moderate, without major impact on activity. While waiting for the national accounts report, we maintain this year<sup>’</sup>s average annual GDP at +2.1%y/y, consistent with 2.6%4Q/4Q.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.
