## United Kingdom

• Resilience in the labour market

Downside surprise in Jan CPI, but underlying details are firm

• GDP contracted in 4Q, pickup expected

• Retail sales rebounded in Jan

While there were mixed surprises in the data this week, on balance, we think they support our call for a later BoE cut in August, where we now see the risks as more two sided. While GDP contracted in 4Q, a strong gain in January retail sales serves to highlight the volatility in these data. Overall, we think the economy is better described as stagnating. We place more weight on some of the surveys and softer data for momentum signals, and these indicators have been tentatively hinting at a 1H24 growth pickup. One of them is the PMI. We look for the survey to show some payback in next week<sup>’</sup>s February flash, but to a level that would still signal that growth is on track for stepping up in 1Q as per our forecast. While inflation surprised to the downside, this was concentrated narrowly in airfares and accommodation, two categories the MPC has tended to strip out. We thought the labour market report was more meaningful, signalling a resumption of firm pay growth after a recent dip and pointing to a stabilisation in the V/U ratio at still high levels. The data need to be interpreted with caution, but on balance should make the MPC more cautious about starting to ease absent signs of clearer progress.

## A firm labour market report

The latest UK labour market report was firm across the board The ONS announced last week that the previously suspended unemployment rate data for the three months to November was 3.9%, which was lower than the 4.2% it had estimated using experimental data. An update to that series for the three months to December came out at 3.8%. Not only is this still significantly lower than the experimental data, but the trend is downward with five consecutive monthly declines. Several caveats still apply, and the sample size of these latest data is still smaller than the ONS would like, but on the surface the latest data challenge the idea that the labour market has been rapidly loosening of late. Although vacancies continue to fall, the V/U ratio has stabilised above its pre-pandemic level.

The data show that the decline in the unemployment rate over the past five months has been largely about a falling participation rate (Figure 1). According to the ONS, that reflects an ongoing rise in long-term sickness. After showing a recovery into 2023, the participation rate has now fallen back to its August 2022 low. In the very latest data, a tick up in the employment rate also helped to pull the unemployment rate

lower. The latest data from the LFS household survey show employment up 0.9% annualised on a 3m/3m basis. The ONS has cautioned against placing weight on high frequency changes in the data, but employment data from payrolls rose 48k in January and with the prior month<sup>’</sup>s decline revised away. These data show annualised gains at a strong 1.5% pace on a 3m/3m basis.

Figure 1: Participation rate and the unemployment rate %, 16+ Unemployment rate
![](images/1ee5991b06cbdde2f79285100358004887e4b0ecd044220cf448698aae645b14.jpg)

The average earnings data were also strong. The prior month<sup>’</sup>s private sector regular pay figure for November was revised up from 0.6%m/m to 0.8%m/m - a clear rebound from the very weak patch seen in Sep/Oct - and the very latest data for December also show a firm 0.4%m/m gain. If pay were to continue at this pace the 3m/3m annualised rate would swing higher from its current 2.5% pace to 4.2% in the next release and then towards 5%. This run rate would fit with some of the other measures of pay for early 2024, and would take on added significance given more people have their pay reset around the start of the year.

The smoothed annual rate of private sector average earnings growth dropped from 6.6% to 6.2% in this release. That rate could now tick higher in the next release. The broader trend is still downward, but the pace is slower than previously appeared likely and at present, it looks as though this measure of pay will still be running close to 6% in 1Q24, slightly above the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 5.7% forecast. We don<sup>’</sup>t place much weight on the PAYE payroll-based measure of pay, but those data have also firmed of late and a private sector proxy shows the median running at 6.0%oya. This week, some attention was paid to the CIPD survey indicating a step down in expected pay growth from 5% to 4% for this year. That would be lower than other indicators available for this year, e.g. settlements and the BOE<sup>’</sup>s agents survey, but the caveat is that this survey had already been running lower than those on average, and shows a similar step change.

The BoE is cautious in interpreting all the official labour market data at present, owing to issues with volatility, revisions

and low sample sizes, but in several of the cases described above, there is some corroborating data. For example, payroll employment is holding strong and other guides to pay are generally coming in within the 5-6% range. The unemployment rate decline is harder to explain with the economy stagnating, and the usually reliable REC continues to suggest a rising trend. However, the latest official unemployment rate does seem more consistent with the stickiness seen in pay measures. If this recent signal from the labour market data is sustained and continues to be corroborated by other sources, it would indicate more inflation persistence and call for greater BoE caution when thinking about the timing of the first cut. In our view, the main issue for the BoE is not the level of the unemployment rate per se, but the absence of a clearly loosening market.

## Narrow downside CPI surprise in Jan

Headline CPI prices rose 0.1% m/m, sa in January, based on our seasonal adjustment, leaving the year-ago rate at 4.0% (J.P.Morgan 4.2; consensus 4.1; BoE 4.1). Core prices also rose 0.1%m/m, sa leaving the year-ago rate steady at 5.1% (J.P.Morgan 5.1; consensus 5.2%). Services prices fell 0.1% m/m, sa, leading to a small rise in the year-ago rate from 6.4% to 6.5%oya (J.P.Morgan 6.7; consensus 6.8; BoE 6.6). RPI inflation fell from 5.2% to 4.9%oya (J.P.Morgan 5.4; consensus 5.1).

Core goods came in close to our expectations, with prices rising 0.1%m/m, sa, on our estimates. This was a second consecutive rise that seemingly draws to a close the run of months from last autumn in which prices had been falling. PPI prices continue to fall, although the PMI shows a rise in manufacturing input prices. With Red Sea disruptions probably yet to enter the data, there is scope for firmer core goods gains in the months ahead. Elsewhere in goods, domestic energy bills rose over the month as expected, while food prices rose for the first time in three months.

Most of the surprise came from services, which was close to being rounded down to 5.0%oya. Over the month, we estimate prices fell by 0.1%m/m, although this was mostly due to a big drop in transport services, which includes the volatile airfares component. This category almost fully repeated last year<sup>’</sup>s larger-than-usual monthly decline, suggesting the move was mostly seasonal. There was a softer monthly print from core services too. It is possible this also reflects a seasonality issue: there was a big downside surprise in this category last January, which then got unwound. The surprise didn<sup>’</sup>t happen to the same extent this year, but there may be some scope for a rebound next month. But, in any case, several categories showed firm gains, with weakness in accommodation (-0.8%m/m, sa). The measure of services inflation highlighted by the BoE in December – which excludes airfares, non-private rents and accommodation, and is designed to reveal the more persist element of inflation – rose by a firm 0.5%m/m, sa, on our estimates. This left the 3m/3m annualized rate at a strong 5.3%, and pushed up the year-ago rate up from 6.4% to 6.8% (Table 1). The PMI prices charged reading also remains firm in services, suggesting ongoing pressures in the sector.

"Supercore" is services excl. non-private rents, accommodation and airfares

Source: ONS, J.P. Morgan

Our new inflation profile is a little lower than before, and still anticipates a drop in headline inflation below target due to an expected decline in energy bills in April. Weight changes for January pushed up the inflation profile 0.2%, due to a lower weight on energy (where prices should fall later this year) and a higher weight on services (where inflation is running higher). We had anticipated a large part of this, however, so the impact on our forecast is small (0.06% on average).

Overall, the surprise in this release was modest compared to the BoE<sup>’</sup>s expectations, even on services. The downside surprise should also be taken together with this week<sup>’</sup>s strongerthan-expected labour market report. Early hints about 2024 pay are coming in on the firmer side. And if growth does recover in 1H24 as we expect, generating more labor demand, this would increase the chances that cost pressures are passed on by firms.

## GDP contracted in 4Q

Monthly GDP fell 0.1%m/m in December (JPMorgan -0.1; Consensus -0.2), helped in large part by the earlier reported extreme weakness in retail sales in the month. The sector details were very mixed, however. Public sector services showed some large declines and while financial services also fell, there were some solid gains in other business services. Services overall fell 0.1%, but with consumer-facing services falling 0.8%m/m. This highlights the resilience of businessfacing sectors. Likewise, manufacturing output was not only up by a strong 0.8% in December, but came alongside a large upward revision to the prior month.

On net, revisions to past months were downward, and as such GDP for 4Q as a whole came in below expectations with a contraction of -0.3%q/q (JPMorgan 0.0; Consensus -0.1) or

-1.4% annualised. With business investment growing 1.5%q/q over the quarter, consumption was the disappointment with household spending down 0.1%q/q and government down 0.3%. The absence of a consumer recovery in the face of solid real income growth and improving confidence is a surprise. Net trade also exerted a drag on 4Q, with exports and imports falling.

Some will argue this meets the definition of a technical recession. It is certainly true that consumption is underperforming in the data, especially with 3Q now showing a 0.9% contraction, but it is important to bear a few points in mind. The cumulative decline is small and the data can be revised signif icantly. Some other survey indicators have not been flagging the same degree of weakness (e.g. the PMI). Moreover, with business confidence looking solid and employment sustaining a high level, businesses don<sup>’</sup>t appear in retrenchment mode as would typically happen in a recession. Indeed, the momentum indicators for early 2024 look increasingly encouraging.

Bailey made a similar point when speaking earlier this week. The quarterly GDP data are nevertheless a downside surprise compared to the BoE<sup>’</sup>s -0.1%q/q forecast for 4Q, but it is important to remember the BoE is focused on inflation persistence. While the labour market has indeed been loosening (as measured by the V/U ratio at least) there are signs that process has halted just recently. Other indicators of underlying inflation are showing only a slow improvement. We expect growth to pick up in 1Q.We continue to think a step-up in consumption will occur in 1H24, and leave our forecasts unrevised (+1% annualised for 1Q24).

## Retail sales rebound sharply

Following December<sup>’</sup>s 3.3%m/m drop in retail sales volumes, the data for January show a 3.4% rise. Broadly the same V-shape pattern can be seen in most of the subcomponents – aside from clothing – suggesting there is no meaningful signal from the Dec/Jan retail sales data overall. Instead, the data appear to be showing noise around the holiday period, perhaps reflecting seasonal adjustment challenges or possibly temporary changes in spending preferences by consumers. Overall, the trend looks close to flat.

We have never placed that much weight on the monthly retail sales data as a reliable signal on consumer spending, particularly around the Christmas period, and this episode has served as a good reminder why. It is noteworthy, however, that Dhingra on the MPC cited the December retail sales decline as a factor in her decision to dissent for lower rates in February, describing the fall as “pretty convincing<sup>”</sup>. It will be interesting to see if her assessment has changed at the next meeting, although the 4Q GDP decline did come in below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s expectations.

Figure 2 shows the relationship between monthly retail sales and quarterly consumption. It is hard to completely dismiss the monthly series: the data feed directly into the measurement of retail output for monthly GDP prints, and there has been a slightly better correlation with consumption of late. The gain in retail sales directly adds 0.1-0.2% to January GDP, reversing a similar negative contribution for December, and this should help to get the current quarter off to a better start. But, as we look more generally for signs that growth is about to step up in 1H24, we don<sup>’</sup>t view the January bounce in retail spending a reliable signal.

## Figure 2: Consumption versus retail sales

%q/q, saar for consumption, %3m/3m, saar for retail sales (ex. auto-fuel)

Source: ONS, J.P. Morgan

Notwithstanding its problems, we place more weight on the PMI as a timely growth signal. A small decline in next week<sup>’</sup>s survey wouldn<sup>’</sup>t be a big surprise, due to the chance of some payback from recent significant gains. But the survey remains high enough for this to still leave it signalling a set up in growth this quarter.

## Emerging Europe

• CE4 GDP at a standstill in 4Q23

• Romania: Easing to begin in May, core disinflation grinds to a halt

• Poland: Inflation falls further, core stays resilient

• Czech Republic: CPI collapses close to 2%

• Israel: Inflation slowed to 2.6% in January

• Russia: CBR to stay on hold for a while

The full set of 4Q23 flash prints for the CE4 region paints an unspectacular picture for growth. In the aggregate, CE4 GDP was virtually flat in sequential terms, at -0.05%q/q, seasonally adjusted (sa), or -0.2%q/q, sa annualized (saar). The print was similar to the Euro Area, where GDP expanded just 0.1% saar. However, whilst the Euro Area print represents a slight uptick in activity on 3Q23, the CE4 print marks a slowdown after a solid 2.3%q/q, saar expansion in 3Q23 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: GDP growth - CE4 vs the Euro Area %q/q saar
![](images/379295e37367031b0e2fbe56f1528175b767008cae8670593ac69b4099d0c708.jpg)

High frequency data shows solid sequential growth in retail sales in all CE4 countries last quarter and we interpreted that as a broad recovery in consumer spending. However, it is unclear if that is the case after 4Q23 GDP data was so weak. Having said that, industry and construction developments were mixed in the region leaving some room for a broader recovery in consumption, not just in retail sales.

Figure 2: Real GDP growth in 4Q23
![](images/d3e4761b8007329452cec5d3ba3b76ca99036b215685732ae42d488e4a16b293.jpg)
Source: National statistical offices, J.P. Morgan

We covered the Czech flash print earlier, where GDP logged a slight uptick to 0.2%q/q, sa in 4Q (Figure 2). It was a good outcome after consecutive seven quarters of contraction, but not a sign of growth recovery. This week<sup>’</sup>s flash releases in Romania, Hungary and Poland show even worse growth than Czechia. Prints at the country level showed flat growth in Hungary and Poland while Romania surprised in negative territory.

The Romanian flash GDP release came in negative at -0.4%q/q, sa, disappointing relative to our (+0.5%) and consensus (+0.7%) forecasts. Also, due to downward data revisions to 2023, the over-year-ago (oya) seasonally adjusted series printed at just 1%, from 3.2% in 3Q23 and against our expected 2.1%. As this is a flash release, factors behind the weakness are not clear. High frequency data shows a significant contraction in IP in 4Q, but a solid retail sales expansion and very strong construction output.

The flash GDP for 4Q23 showed no growth relative to 3Q23 in Hungary. The 0.0%q/q, sa print was in the middle of our forecast for -0.3% and consensus at +0.3%, and marks a slowdown in sequential pace after $3 \mathrm { Q } ^ { 3 } \mathrm { s } + 0 . 8 \% \mathrm { q } / \mathrm { q }$ (sa) print. The over-year-ago growth rate recovered to 0.4%, sa from -0.2% in 3Q23. Non-seasonally adjusted data showed growth at 0%oya from -0.4% in 3Q23. According to the Statistical Office, growth was recorded in agriculture, human health and social work activities and information and communication, but GDP contracted in industry, construction and some market services (wholesale and retail trade).

In Poland, 4Q23 flash GDP also showed no growth whatsoever in sequential terms. At 0.0%q/q, sa, the print was a slight downside surprise relative to our forecast (+0.4%), but outperformed the consensus forecast which was for a contraction of -0.3%. After revisions to the historical series, over-yearago growth improved to 1.7% (sa) from 0.3% in 3Q.

Figure 3: GDP collapse-rebound path in the European context Index, sa, 4q19 = 100 (Euro avg. includes countries for which 4Q GDP is available)
![](images/19403d4dd69b4f05ebc7b18c624536a5671a10bad2f5621230c8b18df2ff85ad.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, National statistical offices, J.P. Morgan

On a longer-term perspective, Poland appears to have grown the most. Yet, Romania has maintained continuous growth while Czechia, Hungary and Poland have basically stagnated over last two years (Figure 3). Poland is the region<sup>’</sup>s growth champion, with GDP sitting 9.7% above the pre-pandemic level. However, all of this recovery took place by 1Q22, and growth has since been stagnant. A similar story is visible in Hungary, where a solid recovery up to 2Q22 preceded a period of decline. Romania<sup>’</sup>s recovery looks more robust — quarterly growth remained on a broadly upward trajectory throughout 2022-23, and sits 7.3% above its pre-pandemic level. Finally, despite outperforming in 4Q, the Czech case remains extremely poor when looked at since the pandemic, with GDP still failing to breach the pre-pandemic (4Q19) level, making it the weakest recovery story in Europe.

Last year was a weak year for growth in the region. Hungary looks to have had the worst growth outcome in 2023, where GDP came in at -0.9%. Czechia also saw a GDP contraction (-0.4%) while Poland was flat at 0.0%. Romania is the outperformer, as its GDP grew 2% in full-year terms. Looking at 2024, we continue to see growth outperformance in Romania relative to CE3, largely on the back of EU-funded projects. Romania has already had the strongest growth in fixed investment in 2023 (8.8% over 3 quarters versus 5.4% in Poland, 3% in Czechia and -8.6% in Hungary). We forecast GDP to grow by 3.5% in Romania, 2.8% in Poland, 1.6% in Hungary and only 0.8% in Czechia.

## Romania: Pulling forward rate cuts

Governor Isarescu explained that NBR needs to see at least two months of disinflation (after January) to be convinced it is on a sustained path and to consider rate cuts. The NBR believe this disinflation will come following a high January print (discussed below), meaning rate cuts are getting close. Isarescu refused to answer clearly when a cut could be delivered, whether April, May or later. He also mentioned that data is released with a lag (the March CPI print is released on 11 April, but NBR meets on 4 April) making May the first possible month for a policy rate cut on these grounds. As such, we now see rate cuts starting in May for a total of 75bp to 6.25% in 2024, relative to 50bp (starting August) previously. Isarescu implied that NBR could have cut rates already in January but the bank expected a spike in inflation and was concerned about fiscal policy. The governor was clear that the main reason why NBR kept the policy rate high until now is because of “major fiscal problems<sup>”</sup>.

If NBR starts cutting in May instead of August and does 75bp instead of 50bp it would not be a big shift in our view and would not carry significant macro implications. Indeed, there is a mild risk of NBR delivering about 100bp in cuts to 6% this year. In that case the effective policy rate would be 5% and, as we forecast inflation at 5.5% (NBR has 4.7%) for end-24 we think this is the lowest NBR could get in 2024. There is also a risk of a rate cut at the 4 April meeting. However, we

Global Economic Research Global Data Watch 16 February 2024

believe they will deliver rate cuts only at rate meetings associated with inflation reports, in May, August and November (in total, there are six rate meetings left this year).

## Romania CPI: No good news

Headline inflation increased to 7.4%oya in January from 6.6% in December. The sequential pace was 1.1%m/m, not seasonally adjusted. The move was largely as expected: JPM forecast was 7.3%oya (1%m/m) and consensus was 7.2% (0.9%m/m). While headline was close to expectations, details show a lot more strength in core inflation than expected driven by services and processed food. Core printed 8.2%oya relative to 7.9% expected, but still slowed from 8.4% in December. We were surprised significantly to the upside by core services but even more by processed food. The stickiness in core services is clearly problematic as wage growth is in excess of 15%.

A closer look at both core goods and core services reveals some acceleration in core goods disinflation but rather sticky core services (Figure 4). Romania has deviated from regional (and even global) developments in core goods. Recall Romania had less goods inflation than neighbours, so somewhat slower goods disinflation is to be expected. The concerning part remains services, and here we are not optimistic given wage growth and expected economic growth.

![](images/de8be8063f231166eca3ed88f0a66c3d43c8d77e3911bc66c9d59a1186f3f14f.jpg)

A sharp jump in processed food inflation together with sticky core services acted to halt the disinflation process in core. This would suggest that the forecasted disinflation in core that NBR has is at risk. NBR is forecasting more core disinflation than ourselves in 2024 and has an even lower profile in 2025. We see end-24 headline CPI at 5.5% and core inflation at 6.4% (NBR has 4.7% and 5%, respectively).

## Poland: Core stays sticky

Inflation fell a bit more than expected in January, mainly due to food. According to the preliminary release, January inflation slowed to 3.9%oya (Figure 1), from 6.2%, two decimals lower than expected (Consensus and JPM: 4.1%oya). As in previous months food prices were benign, advancing 0.9%m/ m (we expected 1.3%m/m), leading to a drop in annual food CPI to 4.9%oya, from 6%. (Figure 2) This explains the downside surprise vs our forecast. Energy prices were mixed: fuel prices fell 2.3%m/m, less than the -1.7% suggested by surveys, but other energy moved in the opposite direction, rising 0.4%m/m, whereas we assumed flat prices.

Core inflation was stickier than headline. Core CPI is not published with the flash release, but if we mechanically extract the contributions of non-core items, we are left with a residual which suggests core prices were up 0.5%m/m, a bit higher than our forecast. If confirmed, this implies core CPI falling to 6.5%oya in January (Figure 3), from 6.9%, marginally less than expected (6.6%). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that core prices were up around 0.4%m/m sa, or 4.5% in annualised terms, a bit lower than last month, but similar to the recent trend dynamics (Figure 4).

We expect NBP to cut marginally later in the year, with risks skewed for flat rates. The NBP will likely continue to deliver a hawkish message, showing little enthusiasm for easing policy near term. In the last press conference, Governor Glapinski said the focus of the MPC will be on core, not headline, which given the sticky dynamics we document above, implies policy prudence is set to linger. In 2H24, we think that absent major price adjustments in utilities (which we don<sup>’</sup>t expect) the NBP could relax a bit, in a context of easing cycles in both CEE and DM, and with core CPI running in the 4.5-5%oya area. We see the NBP adjusting rates lower by 50bps in 4Q (2 x 25bps) to 5.25%. However, risks are skewed for a more hawkish delivery, with rates on hold all year, as recently suggested by the Governor in the press conference.

## Czech headline CPI reaches 2.3%

Consumer prices were up 1.5%m/m in January, which due to favorable base effects led to a collapse in the over-year-ago comparison to 2.3%oya (from 6.9%). The decline was deeper than expected by the CNB (3%), by markets (2.9%) and us (2.5%).

Food prices were the source of downside surprise for us, staying practically flat (0.1%m/m) whereas we expected a 0.7%m/m increase. In the other direction, alcohol and tobacco prices picked up 4.1%m/m, a bit more than we expected (3%). Fuel prices fell marginally by 0.5%m/m, no surprises there as this as implied by the weekly surveys. There was plenty of upward adjustments in other energy though: electricity tariffs were up 12.1%m/m, while gas increased 3.6%. Other regulated prices also were adjusted higher, like water supply, sewage etc., bringing the wider administered price category up 5.8%m/m, close to our 6% expectation.

Core inflation surprised strongly to the downside by printing only 2.9%oya relative to 3.8% expected by the CNB; our forecast was also higher. Thus, CPI data is clearly supportive of the 50bp delivered by the CNB. However, minutes turned out to be hawkish despite the 50bp rate cut. Minutes include comments around upside risks to CPI and some board members linked the pace of rat cuts to FX developments even though Governor Michl stated after the decision that FX was not part of the debate.

## Israel: Inflation approaching the target

Consumer price inflation slowed further to 2.6%oya in January from 3.0%, roughly in line with expectations (JPMe 2.6, consensus 2.7). Food printed below our expectations, energy above, and core items roughly in line. We estimate that momentum in core goods picked up to 0.6%m/m, saar, from (abnormal) -2.3% in previous month — the impact of disruptions in maritime shipments has so far had little effect on pricing, with strong shekel probably offsetting much of the impact. Momentum in core services firmed to 1.6%m/m, saar, from 1.0%. Momentum in shelter inflation picked up to 3.8%m/m, saar, but this was partially offset by another big drop in airfares. Median inflation momentum picked up to about 1.4%m/m, saar, on our estimates.

We anticipate that a rapid economic recovery will lead to a gradual increase in core inflation momentum in coming months, with increased shipment costs and pick up in global goods inflation adding to dynamics. Broadly stable energy and agricultural commodity prices combined with strong ILS suggest that headline CPI should remain contained. We expect that the over-year-ago inflation rate will drop to the mid-point of the BoI<sup>’</sup>s target corridor (2.0) by 3Q and core inflation even earlier in 2Q. We expect CPI to end the year at about 2.3%oya.

With inflation remaining well-behaved and output gap likely still negative, we think the macro picture is supportive of further monetary policy easing. Although global conditions are, arguably, turning not as favorable, strong ILS keeps the door open for further cuts, we believe. We continue to expect another 25bp cut at the February meeting. With that said, we think that fiscal loosening and rapid economic recovery will constrain the space for overall amount of monetary easing this year — we continue to expect the BoI rate to end the year at 3.75% from current 4.5%.

## Russia: CBR on hold, inflation momentum drops further

The CBR kept the key rate steady at 16.0% at today<sup>’</sup>s meeting, as expected, keeping the signal for upcoming meetings neutral. The CBR noted the recent progress with disinflation and decline in inflation expectations, but questioned sustainability of this downtrend. CBR<sup>’</sup>s economic assessments turned slightly more optimistic on growth, with 2024 forecast increased by 0.5%-pt to 1.0-2.0%, which, taking in consideration a stronger than expected 3.6% growth in 2023, implies a considerably higher trajectory for the economy and, possibly, the output gap. The inflation forecast was left unchanged at 4.0-4.5% for end-24 as the policy rate path consistent with reaching this goalpost was moved higher.

The CBR signaled that policy will have to stay tight for longer to secure cooling of the (overheated) economy and a durable slowdown in inflation. Post-meeting commentaries suggested that policy might start easing in 2H24, while the forecast of average policy rate was lifted by 1.0%-pt to 13.5-15.5% in 2024 and 8-10% in 2025.

We amend our forecast accordingly and push back the expected timing of the first cut to July from June, with the total amount of cuts by year-end lowered to 500bp from 600bp previously.

Meanwhile, consumer inflation printed at 7.4%oya, a tad above what weekly releases were indicating. Momentum in headline inflation slowed to around 5.7%m/m, saar, we estimate, as momentum in food dropped significantly. Momentum in core picked up a bit to estimated 6.7%m/m, saar, with core goods sliding further to 5.5% ar but core services jumping to 9.2% ar. Our estimate of median inflation slid further to 3.9% from 4.7% in previous month — in other words, the underlying inflationary trend appears to be easing even as some items continue to contribute substantially to overall momentum. Tighter monetary policy and, potentially, a slight reversal of prior fiscal loosening should help bring core inflation further down in coming quarters, we believe, even as election-related spending and wage increases may contribute to inflation near-term. We expect inflation to slow to about 5.1%oya by year-end.

## South Africa

Moderate fiscal slippage probably kept the deficit at 5.1% in FY23/24 and also FY24/25

Elusive fiscal consolidation places focus on moving ahead with overdue measures

• We look for clarity on the reasons for the expenditure overrun this year and measures to avoid a repeat

Our focus for the upcoming Budget will be on whether it can deliver on important interventions to improve fundamentals, on near-term funding specifics and on clarity regarding the recent spending overrun. The fiscal consolidation outlined in the previous budget (Budget <sup>‘</sup>23: 3.8% deficit in FY24/25 and 3.2% in FY25/26) has remained elusive, as evident in recent high-frequency data (Figure 1). We think this is in part because decisions on the required policy trade-offs to enable effective expenditure consolidation have not been taken, making measures such as the long-promised expenditure review even more pertinent. We expect authorities to pencil in a higher deficit by about 0.4%pts of GDP in FY23/24 and FY24/25, with about one-third due a revenue miss and the remainder due higher spending. This would result in an estimated fiscal deficit of 5.1% in FY23/24 (Treasury in MTBPS: 4.7%) and 4.7% in FY24/25 (Treasury: 4.3%) with 4.1% in FY25/26.

Figure 1: Revenue ratio and rolling fiscal deficit
![](images/ebe4a9e98b5c20160695e5d2092540edbeaef0c37aadb3d802e7d03f6d9ddfac.jpg)

In our view, the realized deficit for FY24/25 ex-post likely will show further slippage than the Budget will project with a 5.1% projected outcome (MTBPS: 4.3%). The extent of revenue underperformance remains uncertain with authorities likely marking down revenue by less than what we envisage (Treasury: -R10bn; JPMe: -R30bn). We project the tax/GDP ratio to ease further to 24.4%, from 25.1% in FY22/23 and 24.7% in FY23/24 as commodity receipts fade. We expect the expenditure envelope to be increased by about R20bn-R30bn in the Budget to account for the carry-over of higher core spending in FY23/24. However, expenditure pressure could intensify further during the course of the year due to required SOE support and higher wage bill growth.

A social grant extension should not present a significant upside risk, unless the grant size is increased by much more than 20%. While the president commented in the SONA about extending and improving the SRD, we think this could refer to making its funding explicit for FY25/26 (it is already provided for in FY24/25). Moreover, the current framework indirectly makes space for policy priorities such as the SRD which allows for a further R2.3bn in FY24/25 and R38bn in FY25/26. This means even an inflation-adjustment of the grant size to e.g. R400-R450 per person per month (from R350) would not substantially add to spending.

Key measures that could meaningfully improve the mediumterm fiscal outlook are the expenditure review to scale down unproductive programs (as signalled in the MTBPS) and indications of a R15bn tax measure which are already embedded in Treasury<sup>’</sup>s FY24/25 projection. Moreover, the Budget should touch on new fiscal anchors (again as the MTBPS mentioned should be included in this Budget) which we think could center on having a positive primary balance on average. Even as we see such a rule as likely useful, it would be insufficient to ensure debt stabilization; Treasury estimates a required primary surplus of 1.3% to steady the debt ratio, whereas we think the required surplus is 2.5%-3% of GDP. This is part of the reason why we project a further rise in the debt ratio while authorities anticipate an imminent peak (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Gross government debt
![](images/b76d8ca8519248c2c9d6408a22e3977197cd7fcb129f7ca875630060f445e8f9.jpg)

Separately, an update on the review of macroeconomic policy may also be included. This could speak to the relevance of inflation targeting, enabling the SARB to eventually move to a point target. In terms of the fiscal framework, we view it as important that the Budget provides clarity on the reasons for the seemingly large expenditure overrun in FY23/24 and how that will be treated without weakening the fiscal framework, e.g. second adjustment appropriation or accruals at the subnational level.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate rose to 4.1% in January, higher than expected...

• ...but the rise appears to partially reflects seasonal noise associated with the holiday period

We expect 4Q23 Australian wage growth to slow to 0.9%q/q sa (4.1%oya)

The minutes from the RBA’s February meeting should emphasize balanced risks to the rates outlook

Australia<sup>’</sup>s labor force survey for January was more downbeat than expected, with the unemployment rate increasing two tenths to 4.1% (J.P. Morgan: 3.9%, consensus: 4%) and employment edging 0.5K higher (J.P. Morgan: 50K, consensus: 25K). The participation rate held at 66.8%, consolidating the prior month<sup>’</sup>s 0.5%-pt decline.

While disappointing, there are caveats which come with January<sup>’</sup>s labor data that lead us to think that at least some of the weakness will be unwound in upcoming prints. Since 2020 there has been a material increase in the number of individuals claiming to be marginally attached to the labor force early in the year (i.e. not currently employed but waiting to start or return to work). The ABS treats these individuals as either unemployed or outside the labor force, which from a survey perspective has inflated unemployment and depressed participation. In previous years this effect has proven temporary, with the two tenths increase in January 2023<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate unwound the following month.

Figure 1: People not employed with job attachment
![](images/b4f8a17a6c3bdc68a3cf87acc307f72f999dbc0fedeeae25f90bb789f414350f.jpg)

We had viewed the January 2023 distortion as the last vestige of COVID-19 disruptions and expected this dynamic to be less pronounced this year. This wasn<sup>’</sup>t the case (Figure 1), with reported marginal attachment the highest on record outside of the lockdown/government subsidy related effects of 2020 and 2021. It<sup>’</sup>s difficult to pinpoint why these dynamics have persisted in the post-COVID world given the absence of restrictions on mobility and work patterns, but the ABS notes future January data will be required to confirm the extent to which marginal attachment is a seasonal pattern, which should help to minimize this distortion in the seasonal adjustment process.

With the above caveat in mind, the details of the survey should be treated with caution given the high probability these outcomes are partly unwound in coming prints. That said, full time employment rose 11K, almost perfectly offsetting the 10.5K fall in part-time appointments. The weakness in employment and spike in marginal attachment were evident in average hours worked which sank 2.5%m/m and are now -2%oya in the past year. Underemployment ticked one tenth higher to 6.6%, but should revert lower if the view on marginal attachment proves correct.

## Aus wage growth slowing sequentially

We forecast Australian wage growth increased 0.9%q/q in 4Q23, below the prior quarter<sup>’</sup>s minimum/award wage inflated outcome (Figure 2) but broadly in line with the prevailing pace from the past year. If realized, our forecast implies the annual rate will accelerate one tenth to 4.1%oya, matching the RBA<sup>’</sup>s forecast and the strongest pace of wage growth since 2009.

Figure 2: Australia wage price index
![](images/2df37181fa0e99a942bb5de0eee6ceac59261edeaba1c823a3ece3c711552f36.jpg)

In the past few years we have argued that the prevalence of enterprise bargaining arrangements and other long term wage contracts have smoothed Australia<sup>’</sup>s wage cycle and capped wage growth relative to outcomes in other markets. We expect this effect will be symmetric as the labour market cools with the increase in unemployment over the course of the past year to exert only a modest drag on near term wage outcomes.

## RBA minutes a balancing act

The minutes from the RBA<sup>’</sup>s restructured February policy meeting are released next week. The RBA<sup>’</sup>s policy statement and Statement on Monetary Policy (SoMP) was marginally hawkish relative to expectations, a tone we think will be preserved in the coming week<sup>’</sup>s release. The minutes have historically provided a comprehensive account of the central bank policy discussion, but in a bid to improve transparency and given the policy meeting is now split over two days and it is possible the minutes are now delivered in a longer form document. The SoMP and associated macro forecasts were released alongside the policy decision and we expect the discussion in the minutes concerning inflation, GDP and unemployment to remain consistent with these most recent changes.

## NZ inflation expectations moderate

The RBNZ's 2Y inflation expectations data moderated further in 1Q, with 2Y coming down from 2.76% to 2.50%, and 1Y falling from 3.6% to 3.22%. The RBNZ prefers the 2Y metric given it is the horizon which policy can reasonably affect, and it is both lower than at the time of the November MPS, and moving further inside the target band.

The RBNZ inflation expectations measure is not the most robust survey given its small sample size, and the RBNZ had flagged redesign in previous releases. Still the direction of travel is consistent with the CPI data itself which (despite consternation in some corners about seasonal non-tradables inflation) was lower than the RBNZ expected, and showed a clear narrowing across the basket, with declines in all measures of core inflation in 4Q (trimmed mean, proportion of items above target, RBNZ sectoral factor model, etc). Speculation regarding potential resumption of the RBNZ's hiking cycle therefore remains puzzling. The reaction function is always somewhat fluid, though at least this week's data add a bit more weight from the data itself.

## NZ CPI partials roll in weaker

January's partial data for NZ CPI (covering 45% of the basket) came in benign, following on from the inflation expectations survey. Food prices were up 0.9%m/m but this is seasonal (there is no Bloomberg consensus expectation) and was offset by lower fuel prices, as well as significant falls in air travel fares, such that weighted average contribution from all groups was a slight negative for the month. In seasonally adjusted terms food prices were down 0.2%m/m, and annual food price inflation eased further from 4.8%oya to 4.0%.

Food has further downside, in our view, to converge to currency-adjusted global food prices; in recent years this group has outperformed in 1Q due in part to labour shortages in the primary sector, though this issue looks less pronounced now based on business survey data on labour availability and monthly job prints in the primary sector.

Accommodation prices are one of the primary drivers of the services sector overshoot in recent year. These were up per the seasonal norm but rose less than the previous January. In other data, retail card spending (a nominal measure) was up 1.7%m/m in January filling after a 1.7% fall in December, to be running at a modest 0.8%3m/3m. REINZ house prices similarly were up on the month (+0.4%m/m) but after a large fall in December (-1.1%m/m).

## Greater China

• China: PBOC details the monetary policy stance

• Next week: China policy rate, housing price, Hong Kong unemployment rate and CPI

The PBOC published its 4Q23 Monetary Policy Operation Report last week, which discussed the latest macro developments and priority tasks for the PBOC in 2024. Overall, the PBOC<sup>’</sup>s monetary policy stance is in-line with our policy forecast.

On inflation, the central bank recognized CPI deflation since 4Q23, and attributed it to a pork-related food prices drag, weak durable goods and services prices, and more fundamentally, insufficient demand and dis-synchronized supply-demand recovery. Nonetheless, the PBOC takes a benign view on the growth and inflation outlook. The central bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast expects consumer confidence will pick up, goods and services consumption will recover further, which will support a moderate upturn in CPI. The central bank continues to reiterate that China does not face inflation or deflation pressure in the long term. In our view, such a benign view may be too optimistic, and hints at a continuing bias of policy support for investment and production with a low probability of direct and large-scale consumption stimulus. Therefore, deflation or low inflation will continue, with risks being more prolonged than in our baseline forecast.

On monetary policy, the PBOC reiterated to adopt prudent monetary policy, and the monetary policy should be flexible, appropriate, targeted and effective. The report detailed three aspects of monetary policy: credit policy, interest rate policy and structural monetary policy.

First, the PBOC reiterated that money supply and TSF growth will be in line with growth and inflation targets. The same description has been used for 2023 credit policy, hence our forecast of 9.5% TSF growth this year (unchanged vs. 2023) is appropriate. The report emphasized the role of both bond and loan components to achieve the credit policy objective. In our interpretation, bank loan growth might be stable or modestly lower this year (10.6% in 2023), government bond financing will continue to outperform, and the drag from the shadow credit and corporate bond components will be smaller (or turn slightly positive). In addition, the report suggested smoothing credit supply. Considering that 1Q TSF typically accounts for \~35% of whole-year TSF, but in 2022 and 2023 the share increased to \~40%, the TSF allocation pattern may return to normal this year, which implies a moderation in TSF growth in February and March.

Second, the report reiterated that interest rate liberalization efforts should continue to further guide lower overall social financing cost. It suggests policy rate cuts will continue, though maintaining the so-called attenuation approach. Our baseline forecast looks for two 10bp rate cuts this year, assuming the next rate cut to come in March. Note that CGB yields have been on a downward trend since early December 2023 and moved lower than 1-year MLF rate, a typical precursor for policy rate cut in recent years (except July 2021, Figure 1). We will have the MLF operation on February 18, and LPR decision on February 20. Taking into account the softer-than-expected January CPI inflation report, one should not be surprised at an earlier cut this month.

Figure 1: China policy rate and government bond yield
![](images/f53410a6fc826718d9034ea3b1cdcf32546ed983f62b03b3dd71f8a592bd3194.jpg)

Third, the report also emphasized the importance of structural monetary policy instruments. The PBOC approved 500bn yuan quota of PSL in December (issued in December and January) to support public housing, urban village renovation and public infrastructure, in line with our baseline forecast. In addition, the PBOC has established specific re-lending instruments to provide targeted support in five key areas: technology, green, inclusive, elderly-care and digital finance. According to the PBOC, in 2023 inclusive finance rose 23.5%, medium and long-term manufacturing loans rose 31.9%, much higher than aggregate loan growth.

On exchange rate policy, the PBOC emphasized expectation management, better monitoring of cross-border capital flows and preventing over-adjustment of the CNY exchange rate. While the CNY exchange rate follows a managed floating arrangement, it seems that the focus has shifted from “floating<sup>”</sup> to “managed<sup>”</sup> since 2H23. By deviating from the commitment to align daily fixing price with last trading day<sup>’</sup>s spot closing price (a commitment the PBOC made in 2015 prior to RMB inclusion to the IMF SDR basket), the PBOC was able to defend the exchange rate without having to sell USD in the currency market (Figure 2). The onshore USD/CNY trading volume slipped sharply in 2H23. Meanwhile, the PBOC issued 160bn yuan central bank bills in the offshore market in 2023 (vs. 120bn yuan in 2022), part of the efforts to manage

Week of February 19 - 23

offshore CNH liquidity and mitigate CNH depreciation pressure. The latest upside surprise in US January CPI report has pushed back expectations of the first Fed ease until mid-year, paving the way for continued unfavorable rate differential for CNY. If USD/CNY tests 7.3 again, we think the PBOC will continue its practice last year to defend the CNY.

The report also included a special feature clarifying the usage of bilateral currency swap arrangements. The market once speculated whether the counterpart countries may have chosen to convert CNY received under the bilateral swap arrangement into USD, thus imposing depreciation pressure on CNY. According to the PBOC, it has signed 31 bilateral swap agreements totalling 4.16 trillion yuan, but actual usage is less than 3% at 114.9 billion yuan. Hence, the utilization of bilateral swap is not a main source of currency transactions and CNY depreciation pressure. In addition, the quantity of bilateral currencies can be adjusted during the utilization period if the bilateral exchange rate is too volatile, mitigating the counterparty risk in the bilateral swap arrangements.

Figure 2: CNY/USD exchange rate
![](images/983cb21abf679b1b051d3ac6afc09137acdb91666b856eec69bc26693a04337f.jpg)

Lastly, the report also discussed financial stability issues. The main sources of financial stability concern are small and midsized financial institutions, local government debt and the real estate sector, which is in line with our views. The PBOC updated the list of systemically important banks in September 2023. There are 20 SIBs (one more than in 2022), including 6 state-owned commercial banks, 9 joint-stock commercial banks and 5 city commercial banks. In addition, the Financial Stability Law will be rolled out soon.

## Korea

• Employment rose in January but underlying trend is still soft

Soft early-February exports data, yet likely to be holiday noise

The growth divergence between robust net exports versus soft domestic demand is likely to continue. While the first 10-day data from flash customs trade data suggest a fall in February<sup>’</sup>s exports, the early February high-frequency data are likely to have been affected by the lunar holiday schedule. Employment increased and the unemployment rate fell in January, yet the January<sup>’</sup>s job growth was led by policy-led sectors and the underlying trend remained soft.

Meanwhile, the Bank of Korea is expected to stand pat next week without major changes in policy signals. Inflation is expected to converge to the target by 3Q24, and we expect that the BoK may trim its 2024 annual inflation forecast in this forecast round (current JPMe 2.4%y/y vs. BoK forecast in November 2.6%). That said, policy uncertainty lingers around household credit growth and major DM central bank<sup>’</sup>s policy stances. On this backdrop, we believe that the February meeting is likely too early for BoK to signal a near-term turn in the policy stance.

## Employment trend growth moderated

Employment increased by 0.3%m/m, sa in January after pausing in the past two months, and the sequential growth continued to moderate to mark 0.6%3m/3m, saar after 0.8\~1.1% in the last three months of 2023, down from April<sup>’</sup>s 2.7% peak (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Employment
![](images/b89c6d40df7ea92c790908f2a7528dc1e3eff622e79760ee47daf7ddc608f3e6.jpg)
Source: NSO, and J.P. Morgan

January<sup>’</sup>s job growth was mainly led by public administration, healthcare and social work, sectors usually driven by a variation in policy spending and insensitive to the overall demand cycle. Outside of these policy-led sectors, the January details were less upbeat. Employment in ex-social private services increased more modestly by 0.2%m/m, sa, with

mixed details. The data details are broadly in line with our view that employment growth will soften in the near term on its path to normalization after a post-pandemic boost.

## Noisy fall of customs exports in early Feb

Customs exports fell 14.6%oya in the first 10 days of February; adjusting for working day differences, we estimate that full-month exports should rather increase 4.1%oya, after rising 18.0% in the full month of January (Figure 2). The February figures must understate exports growth due to fewer working days this February than a year ago (with more working days in January as compensation), as the Lunar New Year holiday moved from January in 2023 to February in 2024. When viewed together, the two months are likely to see about 10%oya growth following 5.0% in December 2023. Seasonally adjusted, the first 10-day exports fell 4.0%m/m, sa, in February, after full-month exports had risen for five months in the past six. We would caution against over-interpreting early February<sup>’</sup>s fall, considering the usual high level of noise in the flash data compounded by the LNY holiday occurring within this 10-day period.

Figure 2: Korea customs exports - estimate based on first 10 days
![](images/6798e3530179c577f4677f6f91785258eb37a534be55ee9216a72e5e850d2439.jpg)

## TOT starts to recover on tech cycle

Export prices rose 2.1%m/m, sa, in January, rising for five months in the past six. Tech export prices led the rise, jumping by 8.4% in January. Prices of semiconductors surged by 17.1% in January, on top of gains of 12.5% in October and 3% each in November and December. Tech prices have steadily increased since September, and their sequential inflation strengthened to 38.0% 3m/3m, saar, in January, recording the fourth month in positive territory after two years of decline (Figure 3), signaling that they likely started the longawaited cyclical upturn.

Terms of trade (TOT) rose further in January, crawling up slowly but steadily over the past five months. However, from a longer-term point of view, TOT has stayed sluggish in the past two years after 2020-21<sup>’</sup>s slide, due to the combination of higher energy import prices and lower tech export prices. While the current level is still only marginally higher than a decade low, TOT is likely to continue gradual improvement, as the economy<sup>’</sup>s key tech export prices finally started to recover, and global oil prices should be stable going forward after the recent stabilization.

Figure 3: Korea tech export prices
![](images/b3c84aedff7bd288fe27b78d194258b06811b876a4d5bf426d4eb57edc3a580a.jpg)
Source: BoK and J.P. Morgan

## ASEAN

• Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s economic growth returns to a normal pace

• Core leads disinflation as oil and food prices abate

• Resilient BOP eases policy burden of FX stabilization

• SBV to wait and see after easing in advance

We expect Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s GDP growth to stabilize at a trend-like 6.2% y/y in 2024. January data conformed to our expectation, as both IP and retail sales growth returned to a mid- to highsingle-digit pace. Domestic activities appear to have returned to normalcy, exiting the turbulence of post-pandemic dynamics and monetary tightening/easing.

Export-led manufacturing should be driven by external conditions, which we do not expect to deteriorate significantly in the near term. Global demand is projected to slow gradually (with a sizable likelihood of a soft landing), while the tech sector should provide a buffer based on rebounding sales growth (our Asia tech team expects revenue to grow 7% y/y in PCs and 2% y/y in smartphones this year amid recovering end-consumer demand and light inventory conditions).

## Growth returning to a normal pace

IP and exports rose by 18.3%oya and 42.0%, respectively, in January, but these striking oya figures were overstated by more working days (due to the Lunar New Year holiday<sup>’</sup>s moving from January in 2023 to February in 2024). Adjusted for calendar effects, we estimate the growth of IP and exports at around 3.5%oya and 6.5%, respectively. Sequentially, IP grew 6.9%3m/3m, saar, in January, stabilizing from December<sup>’</sup>s 10.8%. Exports echoed the stabilization, declining by a slight 6.3%3m/3m, saar, in January, as a correction after robust growth of an average 20%ar for six months until November (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Vietnam IP and exports
![](images/b912149ed5a9d1faf04441cc4f7ba5c89bb65bdf2bde5d7ccacd6efa1eec5383.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

By subsector, tech exports rose a robust 20.9% 3m/3m, saar, in January. Tech shipments recently rose ahead of new product launches in the early months of the year, while they also have been generally on a rising trend since 2Q23. On the other hand, non-tech exports were lukewarm, and their sequential growth fell to a negative 2.6% 3m/3m, in January from a positive 4.3% in December (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Vietnam exports by category
![](images/b352c5bb9a1c432fa23b7bb13e35700cb5297b62cb955d54396f897e6770b4ac.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan. \* Including textiles, chemicals, metals, and furniture.

## Core leads disinflation, oil and food abate

Headline CPI inflation slowed to 3.4%oya in January, continuing its gradual moderation from 3.7% in September. On a monthly basis, headline CPI rose 0.2%m/m, sa, in January, hovering at the current pace for the fourth month, having abated from 0.9-1.0% in August-September. Supply-side factors eased further, as prices for transport (motor fuel) fell 0.5%m/m, sa, in January for the fourth straight monthly drop, and food CPI also fell 0.1% after gradually reducing its monthly gains from 0.8% in September to 0.2% in December.

Core CPI also rose by a modest 0.1%m/m in January, keeping its pace within the usual range of 0.1-0.2% for four months, and over-year-ago inflation continued to moderate to 2.7% in January 2024. Given the diminishing supply-side volatility, we maintain our view that core's gradual stabilization should determine the overall inflation path this year. Although headline may see a temporary hump in 1H24 in oya terms due to base effects, it should broadly remain below the 4.5% target and follow the core-driven downtrend in 2H24 (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Vietnam CPI inflation
![](images/9a333e3c38bf709fde028543f011a1b9ac1e70453110634c85953b61bfaf738b.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan forecasts in shaded region. The dotted line indicates 4.5% target

## Resilient BOP eases FX policy burden

While the benign inflation outlook allows more flexibility in monetary policy, the goal of FX stability has required minimal effort from the central bank in the past half-year, as indicated by its FX reserves staying nearly flat in 2H23. USD has regained some strength globally since August 2023, but it is more modest than in September-November 2022 (when the U.S. Fed was in the middle of its hiking cycle at a 75bp-permeeting pace).

Vietnam<sup>’</sup>s current account surplus has recovered enough to offset capital outflows moving toward the re-strengthening USD, lessening monetary policy<sup>’</sup>s burden in FX stability. Comparing 3Q23 (latest available) to a year ago, balances of goods and invisibles improved by US\$4bn for each. Although the customs trade surplus (a timely proxy for BOP goods surplus) declined recently in 4Q23, it was just a correction already anticipated from 2Q<sup>’</sup>s high, as imports caught up to exports after falling behind earlier due to sluggish domestic demand. We estimate the goods surplus on a BOP basis at around US\$18bn (seasonally adjusted) in 2H23, modestly lower than US\$22bn in 1H23, but still robust compared to the 2020-22 average of US\$12bn. Rising tourism receipts also contributed to the CA surplus, as tourist arrivals almost recovered to 2019 levels in January from 57% a year ago. Net remittance inflows also increased by US\$2.6bn in 3Q23, likely based on the recovery in the number of overseas workers since the post-pandemic reopening (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Vietnam monthly proxy for current account
![](images/b247092cf817be9e4c52da56d3b17eb371dd11d977ba1ece59a8b18106b25344.jpg)

Meanwhile, inward FDI remains a solid contributor to the country<sup>’</sup>s external resilience, at a very stable pace of US\$2bn per month (Figure 5). FDI registration<sup>’</sup>s recovery over the past year from the pandemic-led slump is also an encouraging sign, suggesting that investor interest in FDI in Vietnam remains robust despite the near-term tax increase (global minimum tax agreed in 2024 and taking effect in 2024). As compensation, the government outlined a blueprint to utilize new tax revenue to set up an “Investment Assist Fund<sup>”</sup> for infrastructure build-up, labor training, and logistics arrangement. This could provide enough offset to maintain the country<sup>’</sup>s

competitiveness, as infrastructure and skilled labor were more cited as important factors to attract FDI than lower taxes in a business survey.

Figure 5: Vietnam inward FDI
![](images/329fe177e5ab2c24d938ecefcc5731fcc29314014bc8af2be39e1dbf0e6dd8f4.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

With a resilient BOP, the market USDVND rate has hovered within the range of ±1% in 4Q23-1Q24 qtd with little intervention, unlike in the past, when the central bank had to pare its FX reserves and sell USD to buffer against depreciation pressures on VND (as indicated by the market USDVND rate<sup>’</sup>s being capped by the sell rate at the SBV reserve management department in 2H22, Figure 6).

Figure 6: USDVND exchange rate
![](images/f81f9b93ad303fbbf8492736b4045f3aa52d65f183d03aaa0e91f694bdac9c3e.jpg)

## SBV to wait after easing in advance

Meanwhile, the need for liquidity provision has decreased, as credit growth in sequential terms recovered to a 1.5-year high of 17.8%3m/3m, saar, in December (Figure 7) amid sufficient market liquidity and a low-interest-rate environment facilitated by a policy rate cut in 2023 (Figure 8). Given the current stability in FX and sufficient liquidity, the SBV should perceive no immediate need for policy adjustments, so we expect it to maintain the status quo for the foreseeable future after starting to ease ahead of other major central banks.

Merchandise trade (Feb 15)
Figure 7: Vietnam liquidity and credit growth
![](images/7b55a3fefcea9b1e1f91da67063f37e12dfd2da1990c3e64b056be27736d7159.jpg)
Source: SBV and J.P. Morgan

Figure 8: Vietnam short-term interest rates
![](images/55faf63077b7ed9211e41c1f044641095a3d9c5eaf40524f9cd53719fdc7e531.jpg)
Source: SBV, IMF, and J.P. Morgan

Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s December trade surplus came in at US\$2.0bn nsa and US\$2.9bn, sa. The narrower-than-expected trade surplus came from stronger imports and softer exports.

## India

• January CPI inflation predictably softens, core-core remains benign

• Softer food and commodity prices also drive WPI inflation lower

• IP print surprises to the upside, after disappointing in recent months

• Trade deficit narrowed to a 10-month low driven by lower imports

Headline CPI inflation fell in January in-line with expectations as food prices continued to moderate. While food ex vegetables inflation softened significantly, the decline in vegetable prices fell short of expectations. Softer food prices also drove WPI inflation lower, which also benefitted from subdued commodity prices. Meanwhile, December IP came in stronger than expected after a string of disappointing prints. December IP was led by strong sequential growth in consumer durables and non-durables. Looking ahead, however, we expect easing of government capex to impinge on industrial activity via its impact on infrastructure and capital goods demand. Finally, the January trade deficit narrowed to a 10- month low led by the decline in non-oil non-gold (NONG) imports which more than offset the decline in non-oil exports. This reinforces our view of a very benign current account defiict (CAD) in 2023-24.

## CPI softens expectedly

India<sup>’</sup>s CPI predictably softened to 5.1% oya (J.P. Morgan and Consensus: 5.0%) from 5.7% in December (Figure 1), largely on favorable base effects. In sequential terms, consumer prices rose 0.5% m/m, sa in January close to the 0.6% increase in December.

Figure 1: Headline CPI inflation
![](images/1a55813dc91127a094fb967751db70ea1a14a5bb9aec46b8ab44c349dee26fff.jpg)

The recent run of softer core-core prices continued, with core prices increasing by just 0.27% m/m, sa in January. Since September, core prices have printed in a benign 0.2-0.3% range. Consequently core-core inflation softened further, to 3.7% oya, the lowest in four years (Figure 2). This likely

reflects existing slack in the economy (output is still below the pre-pandemic path), lower input prices and softening inflation expectations.

Food prices can be broken down into two parts: food ex vegetables and vegetables. The former softened in January to print at 0.2% m/m, sa from 0.4% last month led by pulses and oilseeds. While vegetables fell in January the decline was short of expectations. That said, we expect food prices to continue easing in the coming months such that both February and March CPI are expected below 5%.

Figure 2: Core-Core inflation
![](images/2cd958750b732adb6941693e7aa4bc05e724e5e6995ddbb23768a5848e3563eb.jpg)

## IP finally picks up in December

After a string of disappointing prints, IP finally printed above expectations, coming in at 3.8% oya (J.P. Morgan: 3.3%, Consensus: 2.5% oya) in December (Figure 3). On a sequential basis, IP grew a solid 1.2% m/m, sa in January but this was after three consecutive contractions that cumulate to a 4.4% sequential contraction over the last three months.

Figure 3: Industrial Production (IP)
![](images/bcfa1157442bee42fd6c1fb6115c92613311998fa1a0dae65a4f9ed4d532032b.jpg)

Encouragingly, the firming in December IP was led by consumer durables and non-durables. Consumer durables grew 2.7% m/m, sa in December (-2.0% in November) after a series of disappointing prints, while consumer non-durables grew 4.2% m/m, sa in December on the back of 7.0% growth in November. The latter is encouraging and gives hope that consumer non-durables are finally picking up. That said, given how volatile monthly IP prints are, more data is needed to draw conclusions. Going forward, central and state government capex is expected to ease, given that spending was front-loaded this year. We expect industrial activity - particularly capital and infrastructure goods production - to be impacted. Stepping back, over year ago IP growth slowed to 5.9% in 4Q23 from 7.8% in 3Q23, and we expect it to slow further in the coming months.

## WPI Inflation eases further

Wholesale Price Index (WPI) inflation printed 0.3% oya in January, softening from 0.7% in December. Sequentially, WPI was flat on a seasonally-adjusted basis on account of softer food and commodity prices. Core prices continued to soften, contracting by 0.3% m/m sa in January after staying close to flat in December. On a year ago basis, core WPI inflation fell sharply by 1%, reflecting muted commodity prices (Figure 4). Given that India<sup>’</sup>s GDP deflator is primarily composed of WPI, the continued WPI softness suggests that the GDP deflator is likely to remain very muted in 2023-24, with implications for nominal GDP this year.

Figure 4: Wholesale price index (WPI)
![](images/9ab9e4b4a0537ce005aed9678f1ebc597c057401ea02da2071dd75cfede5fa7a.jpg)

## Trade deficit narrows further

The January trade deficit shrank to \$17.5 billion, the lowest reading in 10 months, down from \$19.8 billion in December (Figure 5). After surging last month, both non-oil exports and non-oil non-gold (NONG) imports normalized in January. The latter fell more significantly, dropping from \$40.1 billion in December to \$35.4 billion. This, in conjunction with lower gold imports, contributed to the trade deficit compression. This reinforces our view that the current account deficit is likely to remain very benign in 2023-24.

Figure 5: Merchandise trade deficit
![](images/2693bc21215622d9f66f7820438c650a93ea7aef9e6f5378d9b886311653737e.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Commerce

No data releases.

## Asia Focus: Bank credit

Bank credit generates the bulk of financial intermediation around EM Asia, so its evolution is key to the overall financial cycle. After gaining momentum in 2021-2022, credit growth started easing last year, due to rate hikes in the region.

While credit growth in EM Asia has cooled, China has seen a steady rise in credit as percentage of GDP (Table and Figure 1). This shouldn<sup>’</sup>t come as a surprise, as EM Asia ex-China has seen a large increase in policy rates, whereas due to deflationary pressure China has experienced rate cuts (Figure 2).

Hong Kong, Singapore and Thailand has seen the greatest deceleration in credit amid tighter monetary conditions (Figure 2 and 3), whereas as credit in Korea continued to accelerate (Figure 4). In India and Indonesia credit cycle looks to be turning after slowing in the past few quarters (Figure 5).
